View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Walter and Jesse's friend Mike had helped them in making Crymeth and hence, they wanted to give him a share.
For deciding the share, they both decided to choose one number each, X and Y and found out that K^th Highest Common Factor of their two numbers is a good amount of Crymeth that can be given to Mike .
Walter and Jesse did this activity for D days in total. For each day, find out what quantity of Crymeth they decided to give to Mike.

Input:

First line contains a natural number D - the total number of days.
D lines follow. Each line contains three natural numbers - X, Y and K.

Output:

For each day, print the quantity given to Mike on a new line.
If the K^th HCF of X and Y does not exist, simply print "No crymeth today" (Without the quotes)

Constraints:
1 ≤ D ≤ 10^3
1 ≤ X, Y, K ≤ 10^10

SAMPLE INPUT
3
8 16 2
8 16 4
8 16 5

SAMPLE OUTPUT
4
1
No crymeth today

Explanation

1st Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 8
2nd Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 4
3rd Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 2
4th Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 1

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors that divide both numbers
4. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (in descending order)
5. If the K-th HCF doesn't exist (K is larger than the total number of common factors), print "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand Common Factors
- The common factors of X and Y are the divisors of GCD(X, Y)
- For example, GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Divisors of 8 are: 1, 2, 4, 8
- In descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1

### Step 2: Algorithm
1. For each day, compute GCD(X, Y)
2. Find all divisors of the GCD
3. Sort divisors in descending order
4. If K is within the range (1 to number of divisors), return the K-th divisor
5. Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

### Step 3: Optimization
- We don't need to find ALL divisors and sort them
- We can iterate from sqrt(GCD) downwards to find divisors efficiently
- For each divisor d, we get both d and GCD/d (if they're different)

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
import math

def get_divisors(n):
    """Get all divisors of n in ascending order"""
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid duplicate for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors)

def solve(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = math.gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Sort in descending order
    divisors.sort(reverse=True)
    
    # Check if K-th divisor exists
    if k <= len(divisors):
        return divisors[k - 1]  # k-th element (0-indexed)
    else:
        return "No crymeth today"

# Read input and solve
d = int(input())
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

## Verification with Sample Input

- Day 1: X=8, Y=16, K=2
  - GCD(8,16) = 8
  - Divisors of 8: [1,2,4,8] → sorted descending: [8,4,2,1]
  - 2nd highest = 4 ✓

- Day 2: X=8, Y=16, K=4
  - GCD(8,16) = 8
  - Divisors of 8: [1,2,4,8] → sorted descending: [8,4,2,1]
  - 4th highest = 1 ✓

- Day 3: X=8, Y=16, K=5
  - GCD(8,16) = 8
  - Only 4 divisors exist, so 5th doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution matches the expected output perfectly.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD
4. We need to list all common factors in descending order and pick the K-th one
5. If the K-th factor doesn't exist (K is greater than the total number of common factors), output "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find GCD of X and Y
The common factors of X and Y are exactly the factors of GCD(X, Y).

### Step 2: Find all divisors of GCD
We need to find all divisors of GCD(X, Y) and sort them in descending order.

### Step 3: Check if K-th divisor exists
- If K is greater than the total number of divisors, output "No crymeth today"
- Otherwise, output the K-th divisor (1-indexed)

### Step 4: Efficiently find divisors
Instead of checking all numbers from 1 to GCD, we can find divisors by iterating up to √GCD and collecting pairs of divisors.

Let me trace through the sample:
- X=8, Y=16, K=2
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8
- In descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1
- 2nd highest = 4 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=4
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8
- In descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1
- 4th highest = 1 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=5
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Only 4 divisors, so 5th doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

## Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    divisors = []
    # Find all divisors up to sqrt(n)
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve():
    d = int(input())
    
    for _ in range(d):
        x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Find GCD of x and y
        g = gcd(x, y)
        
        # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
        divisors = get_divisors(g)
        
        # Check if k-th divisor exists
        if k <= len(divisors):
            print(divisors[k - 1])  # k is 1-indexed
        else:
            print("No crymeth today")

solve()
```

The algorithm works as follows:

1. **GCD calculation**: Uses Euclidean algorithm to find GCD efficiently
2. **Divisor finding**: Iterates from 1 to √GCD, finding pairs of divisors (i, GCD/i)
3. **Sorting**: Sorts divisors in descending order
4. **K-th check**: Verifies if K is within the valid range (1 to total divisors)

Time complexity: O(√GCD) for each query, which is efficient given the constraints.
Space complexity: O(√GCD) for storing divisors.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD
4. We need to find the K-th largest common factor (in descending order)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find the GCD of X and Y
The common factors of X and Y are exactly the factors of GCD(X, Y).

### Step 2: Find all factors of the GCD
We need to find all divisors of GCD(X, Y).

### Step 3: Sort factors in descending order
We want the K-th highest factor, so we sort in descending order.

### Step 4: Check if K-th factor exists
If K is greater than the total number of factors, then it doesn't exist.

### Step 5: Implementation considerations
- For numbers up to 10^10, we can't generate all factors by brute force
- We need an efficient way to find all factors of a number
- We can iterate from 1 to √n to find pairs of factors

## Algorithm

1. Calculate gcd(X, Y)
2. Find all factors of gcd(X, Y)
3. Sort factors in descending order
4. If K ≤ number of factors, return the K-th factor (at index K-1)
5. Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

## Edge Cases
- When K is larger than the total number of factors
- When X and Y have no common factors (but GCD will be 1)

Let me verify with the sample:
- X=8, Y=16, K=2
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Factors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8
- Sorted in descending: 8, 4, 2, 1
- 2nd highest = 4 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=4
- 4th highest = 1 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=5
- Only 4 factors, so K=5 doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

```python
import math

def get_factors(n):
    """Get all factors of n in ascending order"""
    factors = []
    i = 1
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            factors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:
                factors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return sorted(factors)

def solve():
    d = int(input())
    
    for _ in range(d):
        x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Find GCD of x and y
        gcd_val = math.gcd(x, y)
        
        # Get all factors of GCD
        factors = get_factors(gcd_val)
        
        # Sort in descending order
        factors.sort(reverse=True)
        
        # Check if k-th factor exists
        if k <= len(factors):
            print(factors[k-1])
        else:
            print("No crymeth today")

solve()
```

The solution works as follows:

1. **gcd**: Use Python's built-in `math.gcd()` to find the GCD of X and Y
2. **get_factors**: Find all divisors of the GCD by checking all numbers from 1 to √GCD. For each divisor i, we also get GCD/i as a factor.
3. **Sort**: Sort factors in descending order to get the highest first
4. **Check existence**: If K is within the range of available factors, output the K-th one; otherwise, output the error message.

Time complexity: O(√GCD) per query, which is efficient enough given the constraints.

            if i != n // i:
                factors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return sorted(factors)

def solve():

    for _ in range(d):
        x, y, k = map(int, input().split())

        gcd_val = math.gcd(x, y)
        
        # Get all factors of GCD
        factors = get_factors(gcd_val)
        
        # Sort in descending order
        factors.sort(reverse=True)
        
        # Check if k-th factor exists
        if k <= len(factors):
            print(factors[k-1])
        else:
            print("No crymeth today")

solve()

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD
4. We need to list all common factors in descending order and pick the K-th one
5. If K is larger than the total number of common factors, print "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find GCD of X and Y
The common factors of X and Y are exactly the divisors of GCD(X, Y).

### Step 2: Find all divisors of GCD(X, Y)
We need to find all divisors of the GCD value.

### Step 3: Sort divisors in descending order
Since we want the K-th highest common factor, we sort divisors from largest to smallest.

### Step 4: Check if K-th factor exists
If K is greater than the total number of divisors, then the K-th factor doesn't exist.

### Step 5: Implementation details
- Use Euclidean algorithm to find GCD
- Find all divisors of GCD efficiently by checking up to sqrt(GCD)
- Store divisors and sort them in descending order
- Return the K-th element if it exists, otherwise return "No crymeth today"

Let me trace through the sample:
- X=8, Y=16, K=2
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: {1, 2, 4, 8}
- Sorted in descending order: {8, 4, 2, 1}
- 2nd highest: 4 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=4  
- 4th highest: 1 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=5
- Only 4 divisors exist, so K=5 doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

## Code Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    if n == 1:
        return [1]
    
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid duplicate for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve_x_y_k(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if k-th highest exists
    if k > len(divisors):
        return "No crymeth today"
    
    return divisors[k - 1]  # k-1 because we're 0-indexed

# Read number of days
d = int(input())

# Process each day
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve_x_y_k(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

The algorithm works as follows:

1. **GCD calculation**: Uses Euclidean algorithm which is efficient even for large numbers
2. **Divisor collection**: Iterates from 1 to √GCD to find all divisors in O(√GCD) time
3. **Sorting**: Divisors are automatically sorted in descending order by the `sorted()` function with `reverse=True`
4. **K-th check**: Simply compares K with the total count of divisors

Time complexity: O(√GCD) for each query, which is efficient given the constraints.

The solution correctly handles the sample cases and will work for all valid inputs within the constraints.

    return a

            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid duplicate for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve_x_y_k(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if k-th highest exists
    if k > len(divisors):
        return "No crymeth today"
    
    return divisors[k - 1]  # k-1 because we're 0-indexed

# Read number of days

    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve_x_y_k(x, y, k)
    print(result)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors that divide both numbers
4. We need to list these common factors in descending order and pick the K-th one
5. If the K-th HCF doesn't exist (K is larger than the total number of common factors), output "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand Common Factors
- Common factors of X and Y are the divisors of GCD(X, Y)
- For example, GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Divisors of 8 are: 1, 2, 4, 8
- In descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1

### Step 2: Algorithm Approach
1. Find GCD(X, Y)
2. Find all divisors of the GCD
3. Sort divisors in descending order
4. If K is within the valid range (1 to number of divisors), return the K-th divisor
5. Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

### Step 3: Implementation Details
- To find GCD, we use Euclidean algorithm
- To find divisors of a number, we iterate from 1 to √n and find pairs of divisors
- Since X, Y, K can be up to 10^10, we need to be efficient

### Step 4: Handle Edge Cases
- If K is larger than the total number of divisors, return "No crymeth today"
- If GCD is 1, then only one common factor exists (1)

### Step 5: Code Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    if n == 1:
        return [1]
    
    divisors = []
    i = 1
    # Only check up to sqrt(n)
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve(X, Y, K):
    # Find GCD of X and Y
    g = gcd(X, Y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if K-th HCF exists
    if K <= len(divisors):
        return divisors[K - 1]  # K-th element (1-indexed)
    else:
        return "No crymeth today"

# Read input and solve
D = int(input())
for _ in range(D):
    X, Y, K = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(X, Y, K)
    print(result)
```

## Verification with Sample Input

For X=8, Y=16, K=2:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: [1,2,4,8] → sorted descending: [8,4,2,1]
- 2nd highest: 4 ✓

For X=8, Y=16, K=4:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: [1,2,4,8] → sorted descending: [8,4,2,1]
- 4th highest: 1 ✓

For X=8, Y=16, K=5:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Only 4 divisors exist
- K=5 > 4, so "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution correctly handles all cases!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD
4. We need to list all common factors in descending order and pick the K-th one
5. If K is larger than the total number of common factors, output "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find GCD of X and Y
- The common factors of X and Y are exactly the factors of GCD(X, Y)

### Step 2: Find all factors of GCD
- For a number n, its factors come in pairs (d, n/d) where d ≤ √n
- We can generate all factors by checking divisors from 1 to √n

### Step 3: Sort factors in descending order
- Get all factors and sort them in descending order

### Step 4: Check if K-th factor exists
- If K is greater than the total number of factors, return "No crymeth today"
- Otherwise, return the K-th factor

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- When K is very large (beyond the number of factors)
- When K is 1 (largest factor is the GCD itself)

## Implementation Details

1. First, calculate GCD of X and Y using Euclidean algorithm
2. Find all divisors of this GCD
3. Sort divisors in descending order
4. Return the K-th divisor if it exists, otherwise "No crymeth today"

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_factors(n):
    factors = []
    i = 1
    # Check divisors from 1 to sqrt(n)
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            factors.append(i)
            # Add the pair divisor if it's different from i
            if i != n // i:
                factors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return sorted(factors, reverse=True)

# Read number of days
d = int(input())

for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all factors of GCD in descending order
    factors = get_factors(g)
    
    # Check if K-th factor exists
    if k > len(factors):
        print("No crymeth today")
    else:
        print(factors[k-1])  # k-1 because of 0-indexed array
```

## Verification with Sample Input

- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Factors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8
- Sorted in descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1
- 2nd highest = 4 ✓

For X=8, Y=16, K=4:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Factors: 8, 4, 2, 1
- 4th highest = 1 ✓

For X=8, Y=16, K=5:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Only 4 factors, so K=5 doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution correctly handles all cases.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor (which is the same as GCD - Greatest Common Divisor)
3. We need to find the K-th largest common factor of X and Y
4. If the K-th HCF doesn't exist, print "No crymeth today"

## Key Insights

1. The common factors of two numbers X and Y are exactly the divisors of their GCD
2. For numbers X and Y, their common factors are the divisors of gcd(X, Y)
3. We need to find the K-th largest divisor of gcd(X, Y)
4. If K is greater than the total number of divisors of gcd(X, Y), then the K-th HCF doesn't exist

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find GCD of X and Y
- Use Euclidean algorithm to find gcd(X, Y)

### Step 2: Find all divisors of the GCD
- We need to find all divisors of the GCD value
- Divisors come in pairs: if d divides n, then n/d also divides n
- We can iterate from 1 to √GCD to find all divisors

### Step 3: Sort divisors in descending order
- We want the K-th highest common factor
- So we need to sort divisors in descending order

### Step 4: Check if K-th exists
- If K is greater than the total number of divisors, return "No crymeth today"
- Otherwise, return the K-th largest divisor

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- If K is 1, we want the largest divisor (which is the GCD itself)
- If K is large, we need to ensure we don't exceed the total number of divisors

## Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    if n == 1:
        return [1]
    
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve(X, Y, K):
    g = gcd(X, Y)
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    if K > len(divisors):
        return "No crymeth today"
    
    return divisors[K - 1]  # K-th highest (1-indexed)

# Read input and solve
D = int(input())
for _ in range(D):
    X, Y, K = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(X, Y, K)
    print(result)
```

## Verification with Sample Input

- Day 1: X=8, Y=16, K=2
  - gcd(8,16) = 8
  - Divisors of 8: [1, 2, 4, 8] → sorted in descending: [8, 4, 2, 1]
  - 2nd highest = 4 ✓

- Day 2: X=8, Y=16, K=4
  - gcd(8,16) = 8
  - Divisors: [8, 4, 2, 1]
  - 4th highest = 1 ✓

- Day 3: X=8, Y=16, K=5
  - gcd(8,16) = 8
  - Only 4 divisors, so 5th doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution correctly handles all test cases!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. The HCFs of two numbers are the divisors that divide both numbers
3. We need to find the K-th largest among these common divisors
4. If the K-th HCF doesn't exist (i.e., there are fewer than K common divisors), we print "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understanding Common Factors
- The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
- For example, GCD(8, 16) = 8
- The divisors of 8 are: 1, 2, 4, 8
- So the highest common factors are: 8, 4, 2, 1 (in descending order)

### Step 2: Algorithm Design
1. Find the GCD of X and Y
2. Find all divisors of the GCD
3. Sort them in descending order
4. If K is within the range (1 to number of divisors), return the K-th largest
5. Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

### Step 3: Implementation Challenges
- X, Y, K can be up to 10^10, so we can't iterate through all divisors directly
- However, we only need to find the K-th largest divisor, not all divisors
- We can find all divisors by iterating up to √(GCD)

### Step 4: Optimization
Since we need the K-th largest divisor, we can:
1. Find GCD(X, Y)
2. Generate all divisors of GCD
3. Sort them in descending order
4. Return the K-th element if it exists

### Step 5: Edge Cases
- If K is larger than the number of divisors, return "No crymeth today"
- If GCD is 1, then only divisor is 1

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    if n == 1:
        return [1]
    
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve(X, Y, K):
    # Find GCD of X and Y
    g = gcd(X, Y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if K-th HCF exists
    if K <= len(divisors):
        return divisors[K - 1]  # K-th highest (1-indexed)
    else:
        return "No crymeth today"

# Read input
D = int(input())
for _ in range(D):
    X, Y, K = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(X, Y, K)
    print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

During a break in the buffet of the scientific lyceum of the Kingdom of Kremland, there was formed a queue of $n$ high school students numbered from $1$ to $n$. Initially, each student $i$ is on position $i$. Each student $i$ is characterized by two numbers — $a_i$ and $b_i$. Dissatisfaction of the person $i$ equals the product of $a_i$ by the number of people standing to the left of his position, add the product $b_i$ by the number of people standing to the right of his position. Formally, the dissatisfaction of the student $i$, which is on the position $j$, equals $a_i \cdot (j-1) + b_i \cdot (n-j)$.

The director entrusted Stas with the task: rearrange the people in the queue so that minimize the total dissatisfaction.

Although Stas is able to solve such problems, this was not given to him. He turned for help to you.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the number of people in the queue.

Each of the following $n$ lines contains two integers $a_i$ and $b_i$ ($1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^8$) — the characteristic of the student $i$, initially on the position $i$.


-----Output-----

Output one integer — minimum total dissatisfaction which can be achieved by rearranging people in the queue.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
4 2
2 3
6 1

Output
12
Input
4
2 4
3 3
7 1
2 3

Output
25
Input
10
5 10
12 4
31 45
20 55
30 17
29 30
41 32
7 1
5 5
3 15

Output
1423


-----Note-----

In the first example it is optimal to put people in this order: ($3, 1, 2$). The first person is in the position of $2$, then his dissatisfaction will be equal to $4 \cdot 1+2 \cdot 1=6$. ## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to find the arrangement that minimizes total dissatisfaction

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's expand the dissatisfaction formula:
- For student `i` at position `j`: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- This simplifies to: `j * (a_i - b_i) + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So the total dissatisfaction is the sum over all students:
- `Σ[j * (a_i - b_i) + (b_i * n - a_i)]`

We can split this into two parts:
1. `Σ[j * (a_i - b_i)]` - this depends on how we assign positions to students
2. `Σ(b_i * n - a_i)` - this is constant regardless of arrangement

The second part is constant, so we only need to minimize the first part.

Let me define `c_i = a_i - b_i` for each student. Then we need to minimize `Σ[j * c_i]`.

## Optimization Strategy

To minimize `Σ[j * c_i]`, we should:
- Assign students with negative `c_i` (where `a_i < b_i`) to positions with smaller indices (lower j values)
- Assign students with positive `c_i` (where `a_i > b_i`) to positions with larger indices (higher j values)

This is because:
- If `c_i < 0`, we want to minimize `j * c_i`, so we put them at smaller j
- If `c_i > 0`, we want to minimize `j * c_i`, so we put them at smaller j

Wait, let me reconsider. Since `c_i = a_i - b_i`:
- If `c_i < 0`, we want to minimize `j * c_i` (which is negative), so we put them at larger j (since negative number × larger positive = more negative)
- If `c_i > 0`, we want to minimize `j * c_i`, so we put them at smaller j

Actually:
- For `c_i < 0`: minimizing `j * c_i` means making it as negative as possible, so we want larger j
- For `c_i > 0`: minimizing `j * c_i` means making it as small as possible, so we want smaller j

So the optimal strategy is:
- Sort students by `b_i - a_i` (which is `-c_i`)
- Put students with smaller `b_i - a_i` (larger `a_i - b_i`) at the front (smaller positions)
- Put students with larger `b_i - a_i` (smaller `a_i - b_i`) at the back (larger positions)

Wait, let me think again with the sign:

- If `c_i < 0` (a_i < b_i), we want to assign large j to minimize j * c_i (since negative × large = more negative)
- If `c_i > 0` (a_i > b_i), we want to assign small j to minimize j * c_i

If we sort by `a_i - b_i` in ascending order:
- Students with small `a_i - b_i` (including negative) come first
- But students with negative `a_i - b_i` should go to larger positions

Actually, let me think about it differently. The total contribution is `Σ j * (a_i - b_i)`.

To minimize this sum, we want:
- For students with `(a_i - b_i) > 0`: assign them to smaller j values
- For students with `(a_i - b_i) < 0`: assign them to larger j values

So we should:
1. Separate students into two groups: those with `a_i - b_i > 0` and those with `a_i - b_i ≤ 0`
2. Sort the positive group in ascending order by `a_i - b_i` (so smallest positive first)
3. Sort the non-positive group in descending order by `a_i - b_i` (so most negative last, which means largest j)
4. But actually, we just need to assign positions optimally.

The best approach is to sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order. Why?

Because if we sort by `a_i - b_i` ascending:
- Students with more negative `(a_i - b_i)` come first (smaller positions)
- Students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` come later (larger positions)

But wait: if `(a_i - b_i)` is negative, we want to put them at larger positions to minimize `j * (a_i - b_i)` (since negative × large = more negative).
If `(a_i - b_i)` is positive, we want to put them at smaller positions to minimize `j * (a_i - b_i)`.

So if we sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order, we're putting negative values first, then positive values.

But negative values should be assigned to larger positions, and positive values to smaller positions.

So actually, we should sort by `(b_i - a_i)` in ascending order? 

Let me try with the first example:
- Student 1: a=4, b=2 → a-b=2
- Student 2: a=2, b=3 → a-b=-1

If we sort by (a-b) ascending: [-1, 2, 5]

Student 1 (a-b=2) at pos 2: contributes 2 * 2 = 4
Student 3 (a-b=5) at pos 3: contributes 3 * 5 = 15
Total = 10

But the expected answer is 12. And the example says order (3,1,2) gives 12.

Let me verify with the formula:
Total dissatisfaction = Σ[j * (a_i - b_i)] + Σ(b_i * n - a_i)

For arrangement sorted by (a-b) descending:
- Students with larger (a-b) get smaller positions
- Students with smaller (a-b) get larger positions

This should minimize the sum Σ[j * (a_i - b_i)].

## Algorithm

1. Read n and the pairs (a_i, b_i)
2. Create list of students with their (a_i, b_i)
3. Sort students by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction using the formula
5. Output the result

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0

for j in range(1, n + 1):
    a, b = students[j - 1]
    # Dissatisfaction for student at position j
    dissatisfaction = a * (j - 1) + b * (n - j)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students in a queue, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to find the optimal arrangement to minimize total dissatisfaction

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's simplify the dissatisfaction formula for a student at position `j`:
- `dissatisfaction = a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
- `= a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- `= j * (a_i - b_i) + (n * b_i - a_i)`

When we sum over all students, the total dissatisfaction becomes:
- `Σ [j * (a_i - b_i) + (n * b_i - a_i)]`
- `= Σ [j * (a_i - b_i)] + Σ (n * b_i - a_i)`
- `= Σ [j * (a_i - b_i)] + n * Σ b_i - Σ a_i`

The second part `n * Σ b_i - Σ a_i` is constant regardless of arrangement. So we only need to minimize `Σ [j * (a_i - b_i)]`.

## Key Insight

We want to minimize `Σ [j * (a_i - b_i)]`. This means:
- If `(a_i - b_i) > 0`, we want student `i` to have a small position `j` (smaller multiplier)
- If `(a_i - b_i) < 0`, we want student `i` to have a large position `j` (larger multiplier)

So we should sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order.

## Algorithm

1. Read input values
2. For each student, calculate `diff = a_i - b_i`
3. Sort students by `diff` in ascending order
4. Place them in positions 1, 2, ..., n respectively
5. Calculate total dissatisfaction using the formula

- For negative (a_i-b_i): we want large j (to make negative * large = more negative)
- For positive (a_i-b_i): we want small j (to make positive * small = smaller positive)

## Final Algorithm

1. Read n and the pairs (a_i, b_i)
2. Create a list of students with their (a_i, b_i) values
3. Sort students by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
4. For each student at position j (1-indexed), their dissatisfaction is `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
5. Sum all dissatisfactions

Alternatively, we can use the formula from earlier:
Total dissatisfaction = `Σ [j * (a_i - b_i)] + (n * Σ b_i - Σ a_i)`

But we can compute both parts directly.

Actually, let's implement the direct calculation for clarity.

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for j in range(1, n + 1):
    a_i, b_i = students[j - 1]  # j-th student (1-indexed)
    dissatisfaction = a_i * (j - 1) + b_i * (n - j)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

Let me verify with example 2 to be safe:

-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the number of people in the queue.

Each of the following $n$ lines contains two integers $a_i$ and $b_i$ ($1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^8$) — the characteristic of the student $i$, initially on the position $i$.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to minimize the total dissatisfaction across all students

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's rewrite the dissatisfaction formula:
- `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- `= (a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So for each student `i`, if they are placed at position `j`, their contribution is `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`.

The total dissatisfaction is the sum over all students:
- `∑[(a_i - b_i) * j_i + (b_i * n - a_i)]`
- `= ∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i + ∑(b_i * n - a_i)`

The second part `∑(b_i * n - a_i)` is constant - it doesn't depend on the arrangement. So we only need to minimize the first part.

To minimize `∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i`, we should:
- Put students with large positive `(a_i - b_i)` values at small positions (since multiplied by j_i)
- Put students with large negative `(a_i - b_i)` values at large positions

This suggests we should sort students by the value `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order.

## Algorithm

1. For each student, calculate `diff_i = a_i - b_i`
2. Sort students by `diff_i` in ascending order
3. Place the student with the smallest `diff_i` at position 1, next at position 2, etc.
4. Calculate the total dissatisfaction

## Verification with Example 1

Students: (4,2), (2,3), (6,1)
- diff values: 4-2=2, 2-3=-1, 6-1=5
- Sorted by diff: (2,3) with diff=-1, (4,2) with diff=2, (6,1) with diff=5
- Position 1: student (2,3): 2*(1-1) + 3*(3-1) = 0 + 6 = 6
- Position 2: student (4,2): 4*(2-1) + 2*(3-2) = 4 + 2 = 6  
- Position 3: student (6,1): 6*(3-1) + 1*(3-3) = 12 + 0 = 12
- Total: 6 + 6 + 12 = 24

Wait, this doesn't match the expected output of 12. Let me reconsider.

Looking at the example solution: order (3,1,2) means:
- Student 3 (6,1) at position 1: 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Student 1 (4,2) at position 2: 4*1 + 2*1 = 6  
- Student 2 (2,3) at position 3: 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 2 + 6 + 4 = 12

Let me recalculate my formula:
- Original formula: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
- For student i at position j: `(a_i - b_i) * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`

Actually, let me directly implement the sorting by `(a_i - b_i)` and see if I get the right answer.

## Correct Approach

The key insight is that we want to minimize `∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i` where `j_i` is the position of student i.

Since we want to minimize this sum, students with large positive `(a_i - b_i)` should be placed at small positions (so their positive contribution is small), and students with large negative `(a_i - b_i)` should be placed at large positions (so their negative contribution is less negative).

Actually, let me think about this again:

If `(a_i - b_i)` is positive, we want to put student i at a small position to minimize the positive impact.
If `(a_i - b_i)` is negative, we want to put student i at a large position to minimize the negative impact (make it less negative).

So we sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order.

Let me verify with the first example:
- Student 1: (4,2), diff = 2
- Student 2: (2,3), diff = -1  
- Student 3: (6,1), diff = 5

Sort by diff ascending: student 2 (diff=-1), student 1 (diff=2), student 3 (diff=5)

Positions: 1, 2, 3

Total dissatisfaction:
- Student 2: 2*(1-1) + 3*(3-1) = 0 + 6 = 6
- Student 1: 4*(2-1) + 2*(3-2) = 4 + 2 = 6
- Student 3: 6*(3-1) + 1*(3-3) = 12 + 0 = 12
- Total: 6 + 6 + 12 = 24

This still doesn't give 12. 

Wait, let me check if I misunderstood the ordering. In the example, order (3,1,2) means:
- Student 3 at position 1: (6,1) → 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Student 1 at position 2: (4,2) → 4*1 + 2*1 = 6  
- Student 2 at position 3: (2,3) → 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 12

So student with (6,1) goes first, then (4,2), then (2,3).

Their diffs are: 5, 2, -1

If we sort by diff descending: student 3 (5), student 1 (2), student 2 (-1)

This gives positions: 1, 2, 3
- Student 3: 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Student 1: 4*1 + 2*1 = 6
- Student 2: 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 12 ✓

So we should sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in descending order!

The intuition: students with large `(a_i - b_i)` have a higher "left benefit" - they don't want to be at the end, so we want them at the beginning.

Therefore, sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in descending order.

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Sort by (a - b) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for pos in range(n):
    a, b = students[pos]
    # Student at position pos+1 (1-indexed)
    dissatisfaction = a * pos + b * (n - pos - 1)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We want to minimize the total dissatisfaction across all students

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's rewrite the dissatisfaction formula:
- `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)` = `a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- = `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So for student `i` at position `j`, dissatisfaction = `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

## Key Insight

The total dissatisfaction is the sum over all students:
- `Σ [(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)]`
- = `Σ (a_i - b_i) * j + Σ (b_i * n - a_i)`
- = `Σ (a_i - b_i) * j + n * Σ b_i - Σ a_i`

The second part `n * Σ b_i - Σ a_i` is constant regardless of arrangement (it doesn't depend on positions).

Therefore, we only need to minimize `Σ (a_i - b_i) * j`, where `j` is the position of student `i`.

## Strategy

To minimize `Σ (a_i - b_i) * j`, we should:
- Assign students with negative `(a_i - b_i)` values to positions with higher indices (to minimize the negative product)
- Assign students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` values to positions with lower indices (to minimize the positive product)

This means we should sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order.

## Algorithm

1. For each student, calculate `diff_i = a_i - b_i`
2. Sort students by `diff_i` in ascending order
3. Assign them to positions 1, 2, ..., n (in that sorted order)
4. Calculate the total dissatisfaction

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we want to minimize `Σ (a_i - b_i) * j`, and positions are ordered from 1 to n:

- If `a_i - b_i` is negative, we want to put this student in a higher position (to make the negative product smaller in absolute value)
- If `a_i - b_i` is positive, we want to put this student in a lower position (to make the positive product smaller)

So we sort by `a_i - b_i` in ascending order.

Actually, let me think about this more carefully:
- For negative `(a_i - b_i)`: we want larger j values to minimize the contribution
- For positive `(a_i - b_i)`: we want smaller j values to minimize the contribution

So when we sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order, students with more negative differences go first (in position 1), and students with positive differences go later (in position n).

This actually gives us the right ordering.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input
2. Create list of (a_i, b_i) pairs
3. Calculate `diff = a_i - b_i` for each student
4. Sort students by `diff` in ascending order
5. For each student at position j (1-indexed), add `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)` to total
6. Return total

Let me verify with the first example:
- Students: (4,2), (2,3), (6,1)
- Differences: 2, -1, 5
- Sort by diff: (2,3), (4,2), (6,1) 
- Position 1: student (2,3): 2*0 + 3*2 = 6
- Position 2: student (4,2): 4*1 + 2*1 = 6  
- Position 3: student (6,1): 6*2 + 1*0 = 12
- Total: 6+6+12 = 24... but expected is 12.

Wait, let me check the example again. The note says order (3,1,2) means:
- Position 1: student 3 (a=6,b=1): 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Position 2: student 1 (a=4,b=2): 4*1 + 2*1 = 6  
- Position 3: student 2 (a=2,b=3): 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 2+6+4 = 12

So differences: student 1: 4-2=2, student 2: 2-3=-1, student 3: 6-1=5
Sort by difference descending? Let's try descending: (6,1), (4,2), (2,3)
- Position 1: (6,1): 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Position 2: (4,2): 4*1 + 2*1 = 6
- Position 3: (2,3): 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 12 ✓

So we should sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in descending order!

Why? Because:
- For students with large positive `(a_i - b_i)`: they have a positive coefficient in the formula
- We want to place them in small positions to minimize the positive term
- For students with negative `(a_i - b_i)`: they have negative coefficients
- We want to place them in large positions to minimize the negative term (since -(-large) * large = large positive, but actually we want to minimize)

Let me reconsider: the term is `(a_i - b_i) * j`

If `(a_i - b_i)` is positive, we want small j
If `(a_i - b_i)` is negative, we want large j

This is equivalent to sorting by `(a_i - b_i)` in descending order.

Actually, if we sort by `(a_i - b_i)` descending, students with larger `(a_i - b_i)` go first to small positions, and students with smaller (more negative) go later to larger positions.

Yes, this matches our need.

## Final Algorithm

1. Read n and the pairs (a_i, b_i)
2. Sort students by `a_i - b_i` in descending order
3. Calculate total dissatisfaction by placing each student at position j (1-indexed)
4. For student at position j: add `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for j in range(n):
    a, b = students[j]
    position = j + 1  # 1-indexed position
    dissatisfaction = a * (position - 1) + b * (n - position)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students in a queue, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to minimize the total dissatisfaction across all students

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's expand the dissatisfaction formula:
- `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- `= (a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So for student `i` at position `j`, dissatisfaction = `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

When we sum over all students, the total dissatisfaction becomes:
- `∑((a_i - b_i) * j_i) + ∑(b_i * n - a_i)`
- `= ∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i + n * ∑b_i - ∑a_i`

The term `n * ∑b_i - ∑a_i` is constant regardless of arrangement (it doesn't depend on positions).
So we only need to minimize `∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i`.

This means we should:
- Assign students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` values to positions with smaller indices (to minimize the product)
- Assign students with negative `(a_i - b_i)` values to positions with larger indices (to minimize the product)

In other words, we should sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the input: `n` and the pairs `(a_i, b_i)`
2. For each student, calculate `(a_i - b_i)`
3. Sort students by this difference in ascending order
4. Assign the sorted students to positions 1, 2, ..., n (position 1 has index 0, position 2 has index 1, etc.)
5. Calculate the total dissatisfaction using the formula

Let me trace through the first example:
- Students: (4,2), (2,3), (6,1)
- Differences: (4-2)=2, (2-3)=-1, (6-1)=5
- Sorted by difference: (2,3) with -1, (4,2) with 2, (6,1) with 5
- So order: student 2, student 1, student 3
- Position 1: student 2 → dissatisfaction = 2*(1-1) + 3*(3-1) = 0 + 6 = 6
- Position 2: student 1 → dissatisfaction = 4*(2-1) + 2*(3-2) = 4 + 2 = 6  
- Position 3: student 3 → dissatisfaction = 6*(3-1) + 1*(3-3) = 12 + 0 = 12
- Total = 6 + 6 + 12 = 24 ❌

Position 2 (j=2): student 1 → 4*(2-1) + 2*(3-2) = 4*1 + 2*1 = 4 + 2 = 6
Position 3 (j=3): student 3 → 6*(3-1) + 1*(3-3) = 6*2 + 1*0 = 12 + 0 = 12
Total = 6 + 6 + 12 = 24

Student 3 at position 1: 6*(1-1) + 1*(3-1) = 0 + 1*2 = 2

Student 2 at position 3: 2*(3-1) + 3*(3-3) = 2*2 + 3*0 = 4 + 0 = 4
Total = 2 + 6 + 4 = 12 ✓

So the optimal assignment gives 12. My sorting analysis must be wrong.

Total dissatisfaction = ∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i + n * ∑b_i - ∑a_i

To minimize this, we need to minimize ∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i.

If (a_i - b_i) is positive, we want small j_i (small positions)
If (a_i - b_i) is negative, we want large j_i (large positions)

- Student 1: (4,2) → diff = 2

So positions should be: student 2 at pos 1, student 1 at pos 2, student 3 at pos 3.

Position 1: student 2 → 2*(1-1) + 3*(3-1) = 0 + 6 = 6
Position 2: student 1 → 4*(2-1) + 2*(3-2) = 4 + 2 = 6
Position 3: student 3 → 6*(3-1) + 1*(3-3) = 12 + 0 = 12

So sorted by diff in ascending order: student 2 (-1), student 1 (2), student 3 (5) - same as before.

Wait, I think I have a fundamental error. Let me reconsider the formula.

For a student i at position j:
dissatisfaction = a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)

The product term is a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = (a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)

So total dissatisfaction = ∑((a_i - b_i) * j_i) + ∑(b_i * n - a_i)

The second term is constant. So we minimize ∑((a_i - b_i) * j_i).

We want to minimize the sum of (a_i - b_i) * j_i.

Since j_i is the position (1-indexed), and we want to assign each student to a unique position, we should:
- Assign students with negative (a_i - b_i) to larger positions (since negative * large = more negative)
- Assign students with positive (a_i - b_i) to smaller positions (since positive * small = smaller positive)

In other words, sort by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order, and assign to positions 1, 2, ..., n.

But this didn't work for the example. Let me check if I misunderstood the problem.

Looking at the example again: order (3,1,2)
- Student 3 (a=6,b=1) at pos 1: 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Student 1 (a=4,b=2) at pos 2: 4*1 + 2*1 = 4 + 2 = 6  
- Student 2 (a=2,b=3) at pos 3: 2*2 + 3*0 = 4 + 0 = 4
- Total: 2 + 6 + 4 = 12

Now calculate (a_i - b_i):
- Student 1: 4-2 = 2
- Student 2: 2-3 = -1  
- Student 3: 6-1 = 5

If we sort by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order: student 2 (-1), student 1 (2), student 3 (5)

Assignment: pos 1: student 2, pos 2: student 1, pos 3: student 3
Dissatisfaction: 6 + 6 + 12 = 24 ≠ 12

The example gives a lower result with a different assignment. Maybe I have the sorting direction wrong.

Let me try sorting by (a_i - b_i) in descending order:
- Student 3 (5), student 1 (2), student 2 (-1)

Assignment: pos 1: student 3, pos 2: student 1, pos 3: student 2
Dissatisfaction: 
- Student 3: 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Student 1: 4*1 + 2*1 = 6
- Student 2: 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 2 + 6 + 4 = 12 ✓

It matches! So I should sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order.

Why? Because the term is (a_i - b_i) * j_i. If (a_i - b_i) is large positive, we want it at small j_i. If (a_i - b_i) is large negative, we want it at large j_i.

In descending order: largest (a_i - b_i) gets position 1, second largest gets position 2, etc.

Yes, this makes sense. So algorithm:
1. For each student, calculate diff = a_i - b_i
2. Sort students by diff in descending order
3. Assign positions 1 to n to these sorted students
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Calculate the difference a_i - b_i for each student
# Sort by difference in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for i in range(n):
    a, b = students[i]
    position = i + 1  # 1-indexed position
    dissatisfaction = a * (position - 1) + b * (n - position)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students in a queue, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to minimize the total dissatisfaction across all students
4. Initially, student `i` is at position `i`, but we can rearrange them freely

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's expand the dissatisfaction formula:
- For student `i` at position `j`: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- This simplifies to: `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So the total dissatisfaction is the sum over all students:
- `∑((a_i - b_i) * j_i + (b_i * n - a_i))` where `j_i` is the position of student `i`

This can be split into:
- `∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i + ∑(b_i * n - a_i)`

The second sum is constant regardless of arrangement: `∑(b_i * n - a_i) = n * ∑b_i - ∑a_i`

The first sum depends on how we assign positions to students. We want to minimize `∑(a_i - b_i) * j_i`.

To minimize this, we should assign smaller position indices to students with larger coefficients `(a_i - b_i)`, because we're multiplying the position by the coefficient.

Actually, let me reconsider: since we want to minimize the sum, and position `j` increases, we should:
- Put students with larger `(a_i - b_i)` values at smaller positions (earlier in the queue)
- Put students with smaller `(a_i - b_i)` values at larger positions (later in the queue)

Wait, let me think about the coefficient `a_i - b_i`:
- If `a_i - b_i` is positive, we want to minimize the product with position, so place them earlier (smaller j)
- If `a_i - b_i` is negative, we want to maximize the product with position (since negative * larger j = more negative), so place them later (larger j)

Actually, since we're minimizing the sum, we want to make the positive terms as small as possible and the negative terms as large (negative) as possible.

So students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` should be placed at smaller positions, and students with negative `(a_i - b_i)` should be placed at larger positions.

Therefore, we should sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order, and assign them positions 1, 2, ..., n.

## Algorithm

1. Read the input
2. For each student, calculate `a_i - b_i`
3. Sort students by `a_i - b_i` in ascending order (smallest first)
4. Assign positions 1 to n to students in this order
5. Calculate total dissatisfaction using the formula

## Verification with Example 1

Students: 
- Student 1: a=4, b=2, diff=2
- Student 2: a=2, b=3, diff=-1  
- Student 3: a=6, b=1, diff=5

Sorted by diff: Student 2 (-1), Student 1 (2), Student 3 (5)

Assign positions:
- Student 2 at position 1: dissatisfaction = 2*(1-1) + 3*(3-1) = 0 + 6 = 6
- Student 1 at position 2: dissatisfaction = 4*(2-1) + 2*(3-2) = 4 + 2 = 6
- Student 3 at position 3: dissatisfaction = 6*(3-1) + 1*(3-3) = 12 + 0 = 12

Wait, this gives 6+6+12=24, not 12.

Let me recalculate with the example solution: order (3,1,2)

- Student 3 at position 1: 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Student 1 at position 2: 4*1 + 2*1 = 6  
- Student 2 at position 3: 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 2+6+4 = 12 ✓

So student 3 (diff=5) at position 1, student 1 (diff=2) at position 2, student 2 (diff=-1) at position 3.

This means students with higher diff should be placed at smaller positions? But 5 > 2 > -1.

Actually, I think I made a mistake in the formula analysis.

Let me re-derive:
Total dissatisfaction = ∑(a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j))
= ∑(a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j)
= ∑((a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i))
= ∑(a_i - b_i) * j + ∑(b_i * n - a_i)

The second sum is constant. The first sum is minimized when we arrange the students by the coefficient `a_i - b_i` such that larger coefficients get smaller j values.

Since we're multiplied by j, and j is positive, to minimize the sum, we want:
- Large positive coefficients at small j
- Large negative coefficients at large j

Actually, the coefficient is `(a_i - b_i)`, so we want to put students with large positive `(a_i - b_i)` at small positions, and students with large negative `(a_i - b_i)` at large positions.

This means sorting by `(a_i - b_i)` in descending order? No.

Let's think: if coefficient is positive, we want small j (early position) to minimize the product.
If coefficient is negative, we want large j (late position) because negative * large j = more negative.

So students with larger `(a_i - b_i)` should be placed at smaller positions.

That means we should sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in descending order, or equivalently sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order and reverse?

Actually: larger coefficient → smaller position.

So we sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in descending order.

For example 1:
- Student 3: diff=5
- Student 1: diff=2  
- Student 2: diff=-1

So order: Student 3, Student 1, Student 2 (descending by diff)

This matches the example solution (3,1,2)!

## Final Algorithm

1. Read n and the pairs (a_i, b_i)
2. Create a list of students with their (a_i, b_i, diff=a_i-b_i)
3. Sort by diff in descending order (largest diff first)
4. Assign position 1 to first student, position 2 to second, etc.
5. Calculate total dissatisfaction: sum of (a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)) for each student at position j

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b, a - b))

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[2], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for j in range(n):
    a, b, diff = students[j]
    position = j + 1
    dissatisfaction = a * (position - 1) + b * (n - position)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students in a queue, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to find the optimal arrangement to minimize total dissatisfaction

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's expand the dissatisfaction formula:
- `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i*j - a_i + b_i*n - b_i*j`
- `= (a_i - b_i)*j + (b_i*n - a_i)`

So for each student, their contribution to total dissatisfaction depends on their position `j` through the coefficient `(a_i - b_i)`.

## Key Insight

The total dissatisfaction can be written as:
- `∑(a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j))` for all students
- `= ∑(a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j)`
- `= ∑((a_i - b_i) * j) + ∑(b_i * n) - ∑(a_i)`

The term `∑(b_i * n)` is constant (doesn't depend on arrangement), and `∑(a_i)` is also constant.

So we need to minimize `∑((a_i - b_i) * j)` where `j` is the position of student `i`.

## Strategy

- If `a_i - b_i > 0`, we want student `i` to be placed in a smaller position (earlier in queue) to minimize the sum
- If `a_i - b_i < 0`, we want student `i` to be placed in a larger position (later in queue) to minimize the sum
- If `a_i - b_i = 0`, position doesn't matter

This means we should sort students by the value of `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order:
- Students with negative `(a_i - b_i)` go to the end
- Students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` go to the beginning
- Students with zero `(a_i - b_i)` can go anywhere

Actually, let me reconsider: we want to minimize `∑((a_i - b_i) * j)`.

Since `j` is the position (1-indexed), we want:
- For students with positive `(a_i - b_i)`: smaller position means smaller contribution
- For students with negative `(a_i - b_i)`: larger position means smaller contribution (since negative × large number = more negative)

So we should sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order - students with more negative values go first, positive values go later.

Wait, let me think again:
- If `(a_i - b_i)` is positive, we want small `j`
- If `(a_i - b_i)` is negative, we want large `j`

So we should sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order - negative values first, then zero, then positive.

## Algorithm

1. For each student, calculate `diff = a_i - b_i`
2. Sort students by `diff` in ascending order
3. Assign positions 1 to n to students in this sorted order
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction using the formula

## Verification with Example 1

Students: (4,2), (2,3), (6,1)
- diff: 4-2=2, 2-3=-1, 6-1=5
- Sort by diff: (-1, 2, 5) → students with diff -1, then 2, then 5
- So order: student 2 (diff -1), student 1 (diff 2), student 3 (diff 5)
- Position: student 2 at pos 1, student 1 at pos 2, student 3 at pos 3
- Dissatisfaction: 
  - student 2: 2*(1-1) + 3*(3-1) = 0 + 6 = 6
  - student 1: 4*(2-1) + 2*(3-2) = 4 + 2 = 6  
  - student 3: 6*(3-1) + 1*(3-3) = 12 + 0 = 12
  - Total: 6 + 6 + 12 = 24

Wait, this doesn't match the expected output of 12.

Let me check the example again. The example says order (3,1,2) gives 12.
- Position 1: student 3 → 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Position 2: student 1 → 4*1 + 2*1 = 6
- Position 3: student 2 → 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Total: 2 + 6 + 4 = 12

So what's different? Let me re-express the formula:
- Total dissatisfaction = ∑(a_i*(j-1) + b_i*(n-j))
- = ∑(a_i*(j-1)) + ∑(b_i*(n-j))
- = ∑(a_i*j - a_i) + ∑(b_i*n - b_i*j)
- = ∑(a_i*j) - ∑(a_i) + n*∑(b_i) - ∑(b_i*j)
- = ∑((a_i - b_i)*j) + (n*∑(b_i) - ∑(a_i))

So the total = ∑((a_i - b_i)*j) + constant, where constant = n*∑(b_i) - ∑(a_i)

For the optimal arrangement, we need to minimize ∑((a_i - b_i)*j).

For example 1:
- Student 1: (4,2), diff = 2
- Student 2: (2,3), diff = -1  
- Student 3: (6,1), diff = 5

If we sort by diff ascending: student 2 (diff -1), student 1 (diff 2), student 3 (diff 5)
- j: 1, 2, 3
- sum = (-1)*1 + 2*2 + 5*3 = -1 + 4 + 15 = 18

But expected total is 12. Let's calculate constant:
- n*∑(b_i) - ∑(a_i) = 3*(2+3+1) - (4+2+6) = 3*6 - 12 = 18 - 12 = 6
- Total = 18 + 6 = 24

I'm getting 24, but expected is 12. What's wrong?

Wait, let me recalculate for the given arrangement (3,1,2):
- Student 3 at pos 1: 6*0 + 1*2 = 2
- Student 1 at pos 2: 4*1 + 2*1 = 4 + 2 = 6
- Student 2 at pos 3: 2*2 + 3*0 = 4
- Sum = 2 + 6 + 4 = 12

Now calculate according to formula:
- ∑((a_i - b_i)*j) + n*∑(b_i) - ∑(a_i)
- For student 3 at pos 1: (6-1)*1 = 5*1 = 5
- For student 1 at pos 2: (4-2)*2 = 2*2 = 4  
- For student 2 at pos 3: (2-3)*3 = (-1)*3 = -3
- Sum = 5 + 4 - 3 = 6
- Constant = 3*(2+3+1) - (4+2+6) = 18 - 12 = 6
- Total = 6 + 6 = 12 ✓

The formula works! But when I sort by diff, I need to get the minimum sum of (a_i-b_i)*j.

For the arrangement (3,1,2): student 3 (diff 5) at pos 1, student 1 (diff 2) at pos 2, student 2 (diff -1) at pos 3
- Sum = 5*1 + 2*2 + (-1)*3 = 5 + 4 - 3 = 6

If I sort by diff ascending: student 2 (diff -1) at pos 1, student 1 (diff 2) at pos 2, student 3 (diff 5) at pos 3
- Sum = (-1)*1 + 2*2 + 5*3 = -1 + 4 + 15 = 18

18 > 6, so my intuition is wrong.

Actually, to minimize ∑((a_i - b_i)*j), we want:
- When (a_i - b_i) is positive, we want smaller j (earlier positions)
- When (a_i - b_i) is negative, we want larger j (later positions)

So actually, we want to sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order? 

For student with diff > 0, we want small j → so we want them to have small position number
For student with diff < 0, we want large j → so they should come after students with positive diff

So the optimal order is by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order? 

Wait: ascending means negative first, then positive. 
- Negative diff: large j (good for minimizing since negative × large = very negative)
- Positive diff: small j (good for minimizing since positive × small = small positive)

But for negative diff: negative × large j → becomes more negative → good!
For positive diff: positive × small j → becomes small positive → good!

Actually, both are good! 

When we sort in ascending order of (a_i - b_i), students with negative diff come first, then positive diff.
- Students with negative diff get smaller positions (j=1,2,...), so negative × small j = less negative
- Students with positive diff get larger positions, so positive × larger j = larger positive

This isn't right.

Let me think differently: we want to minimize ∑((a_i - b_i)*j).

For a student with (a_i - b_i) > 0, we want to assign them a small j to minimize the positive contribution.
For a student with (a_i - b_i) < 0, we want to assign them a large j to make the negative contribution as large (in magnitude) as possible.

So we want to:
1. Assign smaller positions to students with positive (a_i - b_i)
2. Assign larger positions to students with negative (a_i - b_i)

This means we should sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order:
- Students with positive (a_i - b_i) come first (smaller positions)
- Students with negative (a_i - b_i) come later (larger positions)

Let's test this with example 1:
- Sort by (a_i - b_i) descending: student 3 (diff 5), student 1 (diff 2), student 2 (diff -1)
- Position: 1, 2, 3
- Sum = 5*1 + 2*2 + (-1)*3 = 5 + 4 - 3 = 6
- Total dissatisfaction = 6 + (3*(2+3+1) - (4+2+6)) = 6 + (18 - 12) = 12 ✓

Perfect! This matches.

## Final Algorithm

1. Read n and the students' a_i, b_i
2. For each student, calculate diff = a_i - b_i
3. Sort students by diff in descending order (largest diff first)
4. Assign positions 1 to n to students in this order
5. Calculate total dissatisfaction = ∑((a_i - b_i) * j) + n * ∑(b_i) - ∑(a_i)

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
total_b = 0
total_a = 0

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b, a - b))
    total_b += b
    total_a += a

# Sort by (a-b) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[2], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for i in range(n):
    a, b, diff = students[i]
    position = i + 1  # 1-indexed position
    total_dissatisfaction += diff * position

# Add the constant term
total_dissatisfaction += n * total_b - total_a

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

    total_a += a

# Sort by (a-b) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[2], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for i in range(n):
    a, b, diff = students[i]
    position = i + 1  # 1-indexed position
    total_dissatisfaction += diff * position

# Add the constant term
total_dissatisfaction += n * total_b - total_a

print(total_dissatisfaction)

Formally, the dissatisfaction of the student $i$, which is on the position $j$, equals $a_i \cdot (j-1) + b_i \cdot (n-j)$.

The director entrusted Stas with the task: rearrange the people in the queue so that minimize the total dissatisfaction.

Although Stas is able to solve such problems, this was not given to him. He turned for help to you.

-----Output-----

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students in a queue, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. When student `i` is at position `j`, their dissatisfaction is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to find the optimal arrangement to minimize total dissatisfaction

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's expand the dissatisfaction formula:
- For student `i` at position `j`: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- This simplifies to: `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

The total dissatisfaction is the sum over all students:
- `∑[(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)]`
- `= ∑(a_i - b_i) * j + ∑(b_i * n - a_i)`
- `= ∑(a_i - b_i) * j + n * ∑b_i - ∑a_i`

The second part `n * ∑b_i - ∑a_i` is constant regardless of arrangement.
So we need to minimize `∑(a_i - b_i) * j`.

This means we should:
- Assign students with small `(a_i - b_i)` values to positions with small indices (early positions)
- Assign students with large `(a_i - b_i)` values to positions with large indices (late positions)

Actually, let me reconsider. Since we want to minimize `∑(a_i - b_i) * j`, we should:
- If `(a_i - b_i) < 0`, then we want to assign these students to smaller positions (since negative coefficient means smaller j gives smaller product)
- If `(a_i - b_i) > 0`, then we want to assign these students to larger positions (since positive coefficient means larger j gives smaller value? Wait no...)

Let's think again: if `(a_i - b_i) > 0`, then increasing `j` increases the term `(a_i - b_i) * j`, so we want to put these students in smaller positions.
If `(a_i - b_i) < 0`, then increasing `j` decreases the term (since negative times larger j gives more negative), so we want to put these students in larger positions.

So in both cases, we want to arrange students in order of increasing `(a_i - b_i)` - students with more negative `(a_i - b_i)` get smaller positions, students with more positive `(a_i - b_i)` get larger positions.

Actually, let me verify:
- For `(a_i - b_i) < 0`: we want to maximize `j` to minimize the total (since negative times larger j = more negative)
- For `(a_i - b_i) > 0`: we want to minimize `j` to minimize the total (since positive times smaller j = smaller positive)

So we sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order. Students with smaller `(a_i - b_i)` (more negative) go to smaller positions, students with larger `(a_i - b_i)` go to larger positions.

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. For each student, calculate `diff = a_i - b_i`
3. Sort students by `diff` in ascending order
4. Assign the sorted students to positions 1, 2, ..., n
5. Calculate total dissatisfaction using the formula

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b, a - b))

# Sort by difference (a-b) in ascending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[2])

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for i in range(n):
    a, b, diff = students[i]
    position = i + 1  # 1-indexed position
    dissatisfaction = a * (position - 1) + b * (n - position)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need:
- x mod 5 + y mod 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)

This means:
- If x mod 5 = 0, then y mod 5 = 0
- If x mod 5 = 1, then y mod 5 = 4  
- If x mod 5 = 2, then y mod 5 = 3
- If x mod 5 = 3, then y mod 5 = 2
- If x mod 5 = 4, then y mod 5 = 1

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Count numbers with each remainder when divided by 5:**
   - For numbers from 1 to n: count how many have remainder 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 when divided by 5
   - For numbers from 1 to m: count how many have remainder 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 when divided by 5

2. **For each valid pair of remainders:**
   - If x has remainder r, y must have remainder (5-r) mod 5
   - Multiply the counts of numbers with remainder r in first sequence by counts with remainder (5-r) in second sequence

3. **Sum all valid pairs**

## Counting remainders

For numbers from 1 to k:
- Count of numbers with remainder 0: k // 5
- Count of numbers with remainder 1: (k + 4) // 5
- Count of numbers with remainder 2: (k + 3) // 5
- Count of numbers with remainder 3: (k + 2) // 5
- Count of numbers with remainder 4: (k + 1) // 5

Actually, let me think about this more simply:
- Numbers with remainder 0: floor(k/5)
- Numbers with remainder 1: floor((k-1)/5) + 1
- Numbers with remainder 2: floor((k-2)/5) + 1
- Numbers with remainder 3: floor((k-3)/5) + 1  
- Numbers with remainder 4: floor((k-4)/5) + 1

But there's a simpler pattern:
- Count of remainder r (0 ≤ r ≤ 4): (k - r + 4) // 5

Wait, let me correct this:
For remainder r in range [0,4], count = (k - r + 4) // 5

Actually, the standard formula is:
count[r] = (k - r + 4) // 5 for r in [0,4]

But let me verify with small examples:
- k=5: 
  - r=0: (5-0+4)//5 = 9//5 = 1 (correct: 5)
  - r=1: (5-1+4)//5 = 8//5 = 1 (correct: 1)
  - r=2: (5-2+4)//5 = 7//5 = 1 (correct: 2)
  - r=3: (5-3+4)//5 = 6//5 = 1 (correct: 3)
  - r=4: (5-4+4)//5 = 5//5 = 1 (correct: 4)

Actually, for k=5:
- remainder 0: 5 → count = 1
- remainder 1: 1 → count = 1
- remainder 2: 2 → count = 1  
- remainder 3: 3 → count = 1
- remainder 4: 4 → count = 1

The formula should be: count[r] = k // 5 + (1 if (k % 5) >= r else 0) for r in [0,4]

Wait, let me think again:
- For k=6: numbers are 1,2,3,4,5,6
- remainders: 1,2,3,4,0,1
- remainder 0: 1 (from 5)
- remainder 1: 2 (from 1,6)
- remainder 2: 1 (from 2)
- remainder 3: 1 (from 3)
- remainder 4: 1 (from 4)

So for remainder r:
- If k >= 5*r, then we have k//5 full cycles of 5
- Plus 1 if (k % 5) >= r

Actually, remainder r appears in positions: r, r+5, r+10, ... 
So count = (k - r + 4) // 5

Let me verify:
- k=6, r=0: (6-0+4)//5 = 10//5 = 2 → wrong (should be 1)
- k=6, r=1: (6-1+4)//5 = 9//5 = 1 → wrong (should be 2)

The correct formula is:
count[r] = k // 5 if r == 0 else (k + 4 - r) // 5

Wait, let's think differently:
For remainder r (0 ≤ r ≤ 4), the count of numbers from 1 to k with remainder r when divided by 5 is:
- If r = 0: k // 5
- If r > 0: (k - r) // 5 + 1 if (k - r) >= 0 else 0

But (k - r) // 5 + 1 when k >= r

Actually, the count is: (k - r + 4) // 5

Wait, let me check:
k=6, r=0: (6-0+4)//5 = 10//5 = 2 → should be 1
k=6, r=1: (6-1+4)//5 = 9//5 = 1 → should be 2

No, that's not working.

Correct formula: 
count[r] = k // 5 + (1 if r <= k % 5 else 0)

For k=6, k%5 = 1:
- r=0: 6//5 + (1 if 0 <= 1 else 0) = 1 + 1 = 2 → wrong (should be 1)
- r=1: 6//5 + (1 if 1 <= 1 else 0) = 1 + 1 = 2 → correct
- r=2: 6//5 + (1 if 2 <= 1 else 0) = 1 + 0 = 1 → correct
- r=3: 6//5 + (1 if 3 <= 1 else 0) = 1 + 0 = 1 → correct  
- r=4: 6//5 + (1 if 4 <= 1 else 0) = 1 + 0 = 1 → correct

For r=0: remainder 0 appears at 5, so count = 1 (not 2)

The formula should be:
- r=0: k // 5
- r>0: (k - r) // 5 + 1 if k >= r else 0

But for r=1, k=6: (6-1)//5 + 1 = 5//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
for r=0: 6//5 = 1 ✓

Actually, it's simpler: 
For any r in [0,4], count = (k - r + 4) // 5

Let me check k=6:
- r=0: (6-0+4)//5 = 10//5 = 2 → should be 1
- r=1: (6-1+4)//5 = 9//5 = 1 → should be 2

I think I have it:
The count of numbers from 1 to k with remainder r when divided by 5 is:
- (k + 5 - r) // 5

Let me check:
k=6, r=0: (6+5-0)//5 = 11//5 = 2 → too much
k=6, r=1: (6+5-1)//5 = 10//5 = 2 → correct
k=6, r=2: (6+5-2)//5 = 9//5 = 1 → correct
k=6, r=3: (6+5-3)//5 = 8//5 = 1 → correct
k=6, r=4: (6+5-4)//5 = 7//5 = 1 → correct

For r=0: it should be 1 (only 5)
(6+5-0)//5 = 11//5 = 2 → wrong

Actually, it should be:
count[r] = k // 5 if r == 0 else (k - r) // 5 + 1

For k=6, r=0: 6//5 = 1 ✓
For k=6, r=1: (6-1)//5 + 1 = 5//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
For k=6, r=2: (6-2)//5 + 1 = 4//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓
For k=6, r=3: (6-3)//5 + 1 = 3//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓
For k=6, r=4: (6-4)//5 + 1 = 2//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓

Perfect!

So the formula is:
- count[r] = k // 5 if r == 0
- count[r] = (k - r) // 5 + 1 if r > 0

But we can write this as:
count[r] = (k - r + 4) // 5 for r = 1,2,3,4 and count[0] = k // 5

Wait, for r=1, k=6: (6-1+4)//5 = 9//5 = 1 → wrong (should be 2)

No, it seems like: count[r] = (k + 4 - r) // 5 doesn't work.

Actually, let me just implement the logic directly:

```python
def count_remainder(k, r):
    if r == 0:
        return k // 5
    else:
        # numbers with remainder r: r, r+5, r+10, ...
        # first such number is r (if r <= k)
        # last such number is at most k
        # count = how many numbers >= r and <= k such that (num-1)%5 == r-1
        # or simply: (k - r) // 5 + 1 if k >= r else 0
        if k < r:
            return 0
        return (k - r) // 5 + 1
```

Let me verify with examples:
k=6, r=0: 6//5 = 1 ✓
k=6, r=1: (6-1)//5 + 1 = 5//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
k=6, r=2: (6-2)//5 + 1 = 4//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓
k=6, r=3: (6-3)//5 + 1 = 3//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓
k=6, r=4: (6-4)//5 + 1 = 2//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓

k=5, r=0: 5//5 = 1 ✓
k=5, r=1: (5-1)//5 + 1 = 4//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 1)
k=5, r=2: (5-2)//5 + 1 = 3//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 2)
k=5, r=3: (5-3)//5 + 1 = 2//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 3)
k=5, r=4: (5-4)//5 + 1 = 1//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 4)

k=4, r=0: 4//5 = 0 ✓
k=4, r=1: (4-1)//5 + 1 = 3//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 1)
k=4, r=2: (4-2)//5 + 1 = 2//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 2)
k=4, r=3: (4-3)//5 + 1 = 1//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 3)
k=4, r=4: (4-4)//5 + 1 = 0//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (only 4)

Perfect!

## Algorithm

1. Calculate count of numbers in [1,n] with remainder r when divided by 5 for r in {0,1,2,3,4}
2. Calculate count of numbers in [1,m] with remainder r when divided by 5 for r in {0,1,2,3,4}
3. For each r in {0,1,2,3,4}, add count_n[r] * count_m[(5-r) % 5] to answer

Since (5-r)%5 = 5-r when r≠0, and 0 when r=0.

So:
- r=0: count_n[0] * count_m[0]
- r=1: count_n[1] * count_m[4]
- r=2: count_n[2] * count_m[3]
- r=3: count_n[3] * count_m[2]
- r=4: count_n[4] * count_m[1]

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

def count_remainder(k, r):
    if r == 0:
        return k // 5
    else:
        if k < r:
            return 0
        return (k - r) // 5 + 1

# Count remainders for n
count_n = [0] * 5
count_n[0] = n // 5
for r in range(1, 5):
    count_n[r] = (n - r) // 5 + 1

# Count remainders for m
count_m = [0] * 5
count_m[0] = m // 5
for r in range(1, 5):
    count_m[r] = (m - r) // 5 + 1

# Count pairs where (x+y) % 5 == 0
result = 0
result += count_n[0] * count_m[0]  # both remainder 0
result += count_n[1] * count_m[4]  # x rem 1, y rem 4
result += count_n[2] * count_m[3]  # x rem 2, y rem 3
result += count_n[3] * count_m[2]  # x rem 3, y rem 2
result += count_n[4] * count_m[1]  # x rem 4, y rem 1

print(result)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

After finishing eating her bun, Alyona came up with two integers n and m. She decided to write down two columns of integers — the first column containing integers from 1 to n and the second containing integers from 1 to m. Now the girl wants to count how many pairs of integers she can choose, one from the first column and the other from the second column, such that their sum is divisible by 5.

Formally, Alyona wants to count the number of pairs of integers (x, y) such that 1 ≤ x ≤ n, 1 ≤ y ≤ m and $(x + y) \operatorname{mod} 5$ equals 0.

As usual, Alyona has some troubles and asks you to help.


-----Input-----

The only line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 1 000 000).


-----Output-----

Print the only integer — the number of pairs of integers (x, y) such that 1 ≤ x ≤ n, 1 ≤ y ≤ m and (x + y) is divisible by 5.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need (x + y) ≡ 0 (mod 5), which means:
- y ≡ (-x) (mod 5) 
- y ≡ (5 - (x mod 5)) (mod 5)

So for each x, we need to find how many y values in [1, m] satisfy y ≡ (5 - (x mod 5)) (mod 5).

## Approach

Instead of checking every pair (which would be O(n*m)), we can:
1. Count how many numbers in [1, n] have each remainder when divided by 5
2. Count how many numbers in [1, m] have each remainder when divided by 5
3. For each remainder r, pair numbers with remainder r in first array with numbers with remainder (5-r) in second array

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Count remainders in first array (1 to n)**:
   - Numbers with remainder 0: ⌊n/5⌋
   - Numbers with remainder 1: ⌊(n+4)/5⌋ (or ⌊n/5⌋ + 1 if n%5 ≥ 1) 
   - Numbers with remainder 2: ⌊(n+3)/5⌋
   - Numbers with remainder 3: ⌊(n+2)/5⌋
   - Numbers with remainder 4: ⌊(n+1)/5⌋

2. **Count remainders in second array (1 to m)**:
   - Similar calculation for remainders 0,1,2,3,4

3. **Calculate pairs**:
   - Count of pairs where x ≡ 0 and y ≡ 0: count_0_x × count_0_y
   - Count of pairs where x ≡ 1 and y ≡ 4: count_1_x × count_4_y
   - Count of pairs where x ≡ 2 and y ≡ 3: count_2_x × count_3_y
   - Count of pairs where x ≡ 3 and y ≡ 2: count_3_x × count_2_y
   - Count of pairs where x ≡ 4 and y ≡ 1: count_4_x × count_1_y

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders when dividing by 5 for numbers 1 to n
count_x = [0] * 5
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    count_x[i % 5] += 1

# Count remainders when dividing by 5 for numbers 1 to m
count_y = [0] * 5
for i in range(1, m + 1):
    count_y[i % 5] += 1

# Count valid pairs where (x + y) % 5 == 0
# This happens when:
# (x % 5 + y % 5) % 5 == 0
# So: (x % 5, y % 5) pairs: (0,0), (1,4), (2,3), (3,2), (4,1)

result = 0
result += count_x[0] * count_y[0]  # (0,0)
result += count_x[1] * count_y[4]  # (1,4)
result += count_x[2] * count_y[3]  # (2,3)
result += count_x[3] * count_y[2]  # (3,2)
result += count_x[4] * count_y[1]  # (4,1)

print(result)
```

Wait, I can optimize this. Instead of loops, I can calculate the counts directly:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for 1 to n
count_x = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_x[r] = n // 5
    if r < n % 5:
        count_x[r] += 1

# Count remainders for 1 to m
count_y = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_y[r] = m // 5
    if r < m % 5:
        count_y[r] += 1

# Count valid pairs
result = 0
result += count_x[0] * count_y[0]  # (0,0)
result += count_x[1] * count_y[4]  # (1,4)
result += count_x[2] * count_y[3]  # (2,3)
result += count_x[3] * count_y[2]  # (3,2)
result += count_x[4] * count_y[1]  # (4,1)

print(result)
```

Actually, let me verify this with the first example:
- n=6, m=12
