- For y in 1..12: remainders are [1,2,3,4,0,1,2,3,4,0,1,2] → count_y: rem0=2, rem1=3, rem2=3, rem3=2, rem4=2
- Pairs: (0,0): 1×2 = 2, (1,4): 2×2 = 4, (2,3): 1×2 = 2, (3,2): 1×3 = 3, (4,1): 1×3 = 3
- Total: 2+4+2+3+3 = 14 ✓

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for numbers 1 to n
count_x = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_x[r] = n // 5
    if r < n % 5:
        count_x[r] += 1

# Count remainders for numbers 1 to m
count_y = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_y[r] = m // 5
    if r < m % 5:
        count_y[r] += 1

# Count pairs where (x + y) % 5 == 0
result = count_x[0] * count_y[0] + count_x[1] * count_y[4] + \
          count_x[2] * count_y[3] + count_x[3] * count_y[2] + \
          count_x[4] * count_y[1]

print(result)
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need:
- x mod 5 + y mod 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)

This means:
- If x mod 5 = 0, then y mod 5 should be 0
- If x mod 5 = 1, then y mod 5 should be 4  
- If x mod 5 = 2, then y mod 5 should be 3
- If x mod 5 = 3, then y mod 5 should be 2
- If x mod 5 = 4, then y mod 5 should be 1

   - We need to be more precise

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for first column (1 to n)
count_0_n = n // 5
count_1_n = (n - 1) // 5 + 1
count_2_n = (n - 2) // 5 + 1
count_3_n = (n - 3) // 5 + 1
count_4_n = (n - 4) // 5 + 1

# Count remainders for second column (1 to m)
count_0_m = m // 5
count_1_m = (m - 1) // 5 + 1
count_2_m = (m - 2) // 5 + 1
count_3_m = (m - 3) // 5 + 1
count_4_m = (m - 4) // 5 + 1

# Calculate pairs where (x+y) mod 5 = 0
pairs = (count_0_n * count_0_m + 
         count_1_n * count_4_m + 
         count_2_n * count_3_m + 
         count_3_n * count_2_m + 
         count_4_n * count_1_m)

print(pairs)
```

The solution has O(1) time complexity and works for the given constraints.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) % 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need (x + y) ≡ 0 (mod 5).

This can be rewritten as: y ≡ (-x) (mod 5), or equivalently y ≡ (5 - (x % 5)) % 5 (mod 5).

So for each remainder r that x can have when divided by 5, we need y to have remainder (5 - r) % 5 when divided by 5.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Count remainders**: For numbers 1 to n, count how many have each remainder when divided by 5 (remainders 0, 1, 2, 3, 4).

2. **Count remainders**: For numbers 1 to m, count how many have each remainder when divided by 5.

3. **Match remainders**: For each remainder r (0, 1, 2, 3, 4), count how many numbers in range 1 to n have remainder r, and how many in range 1 to m have remainder (5-r) % 5. Multiply these counts and add to the total.

4. **Alternative approach**: Instead of counting remainders, we can use the fact that:
   - Count of numbers ≤ n with remainder 0 when divided by 5 = n // 5
   - Count of numbers ≤ n with remainder 1 when divided by 5 = (n + 4) // 5 - n // 5
   - And so on...

But let me think of a simpler pattern:
- Numbers with remainder 0: floor(n/5)
- Numbers with remainder 1: floor((n+4)/5) - floor(n/5)
- Numbers with remainder 2: floor((n+3)/5) - floor(n/5)
- Numbers with remainder 3: floor((n+2)/5) - floor(n/5)
- Numbers with remainder 4: floor((n+1)/5) - floor(n/5)

Actually, simpler: for remainder r, count = (n - r + 5) // 5 if r > 0, else n // 5.

Better approach: 
- Numbers with remainder r: (n - r + 5) // 5 if r > 0, else n // 5
- Actually: count of numbers ≤ n with remainder r = (n - r + 5) // 5

Wait, let me verify: for n=6, r=1: numbers are 1,6, so count should be 2.
(6-1+5)//5 = 10//5 = 2 ✓
For r=0: (6-0+5)//5 = 11//5 = 2, but numbers 5 only → should be 1.
I made a mistake.

Correct formula: numbers with remainder r is:
- If r = 0: n // 5
- If r > 0: (n - r) // 5 + 1 if (n - r) >= 0, else 0

Actually, simpler: count = (n + (5 - r)) // 5 for r = 0,1,2,3,4?

For r=0: (n+5)//5 = n//5 (since n+5 ≡ n (mod 5))
For r=1: (n+4)//5
For r=2: (n+3)//5
For r=3: (n+2)//5  
For r=4: (n+1)//5

Let me verify with n=6:
- r=0: (6+5)//5 = 11//5 = 2 → should be 1 (only 5) → wrong!

Correct formula: 
- For remainder r: the count is (n - r + 5) // 5 when r > 0, and n // 5 when r = 0?
- For r=1: (6-1+5)//5 = 10//5 = 2 → correct (1,6)
- For r=2: (6-2+5)//5 = 9//5 = 1 → correct (2)
- For r=3: (6-3+5)//5 = 8//5 = 1 → correct (3)
- For r=4: (6-4+5)//5 = 7//5 = 1 → correct (4)
- For r=0: 6//5 = 1 → correct (5)

Perfect! So for r > 0: count = (n - r + 5) // 5
For r = 0: count = n // 5

But wait: (n - r + 5) // 5 = (n + 5 - r) // 5

Since we want (x + y) % 5 = 0, we need:
- If x % 5 = r, then y % 5 = (5 - r) % 5

So for each r in {0,1,2,3,4}:
- Count of x with x % 5 = r: 
  - if r = 0: count_x = n // 5
  - if r > 0: count_x = (n - r + 5) // 5
- Count of y with y % 5 = (5 - r) % 5:
  - if (5-r) % 5 = 0: count_y = m // 5
  - if (5-r) % 5 > 0: count_y = (m - ((5-r) % 5) + 5) // 5

Since (5-r) % 5 = (5-r) when r ≠ 0, and 0 when r = 0:
- For r = 0: we need y % 5 = 0 → count_y = m // 5
- For r = 1: we need y % 5 = 4 → count_y = (m - 4 + 5) // 5 = (m + 1) // 5
- For r = 2: we need y % 5 = 3 → count_y = (m - 3 + 5) // 5 = (m + 2) // 5
- For r = 3: we need y % 5 = 2 → count_y = (m - 2 + 5) // 5 = (m + 3) // 5
- For r = 4: we need y % 5 = 1 → count_y = (m - 1 + 5) // 5 = (m + 4) // 5

So total pairs = sum over r=0 to 4 of (count_x for r) × (count_y for (5-r)%5)

Let me verify with the first example: n=6, m=12

For r=0:
- count_x = 6 // 5 = 1
- count_y = 12 // 5 = 2
- pairs = 1 × 2 = 2

For r=1:
- count_x = (6-1+5) // 5 = 10 // 5 = 2  
- count_y = (12+1) // 5 = 13 // 5 = 2
- pairs = 2 × 2 = 4

For r=2:
- count_x = (6-2+5) // 5 = 9 // 5 = 1
- count_y = (12+2) // 5 = 14 // 5 = 2
- pairs = 1 × 2 = 2

For r=3:
- count_x = (6-3+5) // 5 = 8 // 5 = 1
- count_y = (12+3) // 5 = 15 // 5 = 3
- pairs = 1 × 3 = 3

For r=4:
- count_x = (6-4+5) // 5 = 7 // 5 = 1
- count_y = (12+4) // 5 = 16 // 5 = 3
- pairs = 1 × 3 = 3

Total = 2 + 4 + 2 + 3 + 3 = 14 ✓

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

total = 0

# Count for remainder 0
count_x_0 = n // 5
count_y_0 = m // 5
total += count_x_0 * count_y_0

# Count for remainder 1
count_x_1 = (n - 1 + 5) // 5
count_y_4 = (m - 4 + 5) // 5
total += count_x_1 * count_y_4

# Count for remainder 2
count_x_2 = (n - 2 + 5) // 5
count_y_3 = (m - 3 + 5) // 5
total += count_x_2 * count_y_3

# Count for remainder 3
count_x_3 = (n - 3 + 5) // 5
count_y_2 = (m - 2 + 5) // 5
total += count_x_3 * count_y_2

# Count for remainder 4
count_x_4 = (n - 4 + 5) // 5
count_y_1 = (m - 1 + 5) // 5
total += count_x_4 * count_y_1

print(total)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need:
- x mod 5 + y mod 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)

This means the remainders when x and y are divided by 5 should sum to 0 mod 5.

So possible pairs of remainders:
- (0, 0): 0 + 0 = 0 ≡ 0 (mod 5)
- (1, 4): 1 + 4 = 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)
- (2, 3): 2 + 3 = 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)
- (3, 2): 3 + 2 = 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)
- (4, 1): 4 + 1 = 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Count how many numbers from 1 to n have each remainder when divided by 5
2. Count how many numbers from 1 to m have each remainder when divided by 5
3. For each valid remainder pair, multiply the counts and add to result

## Counting remainders

For numbers from 1 to k:
- Remainder 0: numbers like 5, 10, 15, ... → count = k // 5
- Remainder 1: numbers like 1, 6, 11, ... → count = (k + 4) // 5
- Remainder 2: numbers like 2, 7, 12, ... → count = (k + 3) // 5  
- Remainder 3: numbers like 3, 8, 13, ... → count = (k + 2) // 5
- Remainder 4: numbers like 4, 9, 14, ... → count = (k + 1) // 5

Actually, a simpler way:
- Count of numbers with remainder r in [1,k] = (k - r) // 5 + 1 if r ≤ k, else 0
- More simply: count = (k + (5 - r)) // 5

Wait, let me think again:
For remainder r (0 ≤ r ≤ 4):
- The sequence is r, r+5, r+10, ... 
- First term ≥ 1: if r = 0, it's 5, 10, 15, ... 
- For r = 0: numbers are 5, 10, 15, ... → count = k // 5
- For r = 1: numbers are 1, 6, 11, ... → count = (k - 1) // 5 + 1
- For r = 2: numbers are 2, 7, 12, ... → count = (k - 2) // 5 + 1
- For r = 3: numbers are 3, 8, 13, ... → count = (k - 3) // 5 + 1
- For r = 4: numbers are 4, 9, 14, ... → count = (k - 4) // 5 + 1

Actually: count = (k - r + 5) // 5 if r > 0, and k // 5 if r = 0.

Simpler: count = (k + (5 - r)) // 5

Wait, let me verify:
- r = 0: (k + 5) // 5 = k // 5 + 1? No, (k + 5) // 5 = k // 5 + 1 only if k % 5 == 0?

Better approach: the count of numbers ≤ k with remainder r is:
- floor((k - r) / 5) + 1 if r ≤ k, else 0
- But if r = 0, it's floor(k / 5)

Actually: count = (k - r + 5) // 5 for r > 0, and k // 5 for r = 0.

Even simpler: count = (k + (5 - r)) // 5 works for all r?

Let me check with k=6, r=1: should be 1,6 → count=2
(6 + 4) // 5 = 10 // 5 = 2 ✓

k=6, r=2: should be 2,7 → only 2 → count=1  
(6 + 3) // 5 = 9 // 5 = 1 ✓

k=6, r=3: should be 3,8 → only 3 → count=1  
(6 + 2) // 5 = 8 // 5 = 1 ✓

k=6, r=4: should be 4,9 → only 4 → count=1  
(6 + 1) // 5 = 7 // 5 = 1 ✓

k=6, r=0: should be 5,10 → only 5 → count=1  
(6 + 5) // 5 = 11 // 5 = 2 ✗ (should be 1)

Hmm, it doesn't work for r=0.

For r=0: numbers are 5,10,15,... → count = k // 5

For r>0: numbers are r, r+5, r+10,... → count = (k - r) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 5) // 5

For r=0: (k - 0) // 5 = k // 5

So:
- remainder 0: count = k // 5
- remainder r (1 ≤ r ≤ 4): count = (k - r) // 5 + 1

Wait, (k - r) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 5) // 5

Let me verify: k=6, r=1: (6-1)//5 + 1 = 5//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
k=6, r=2: (6-2)//5 + 1 = 4//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓
k=6, r=3: (6-3)//5 + 1 = 3//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓
k=6, r=4: (6-4)//5 + 1 = 2//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓

For r=0: 6//5 = 1 ✓

Perfect.

## Algorithm

1. Count remainders for n: count_n[r] for r in 0,1,2,3,4
2. Count remainders for m: count_m[r] for r in 0,1,2,3,4
3. Sum up pairs where (r1 + r2) % 5 == 0:
   - (0,0): count_n[0] * count_m[0]
   - (1,4): count_n[1] * count_m[4]
   - (2,3): count_n[2] * count_m[3]
   - (3,2): count_n[3] * count_m[2]
   - (4,1): count_n[4] * count_m[1]

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for n
count_n = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    if r == 0:
        count_n[r] = n // 5
    else:
        count_n[r] = (n - r) // 5 + 1

# Count remainders for m
count_m = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    if r == 0:
        count_m[r] = m // 5
    else:
        count_m[r] = (m - r) // 5 + 1

# Count pairs where (x+y) % 5 == 0
result = 0
result += count_n[0] * count_m[0]  # (0,0)
result += count_n[1] * count_m[4]  # (1,4)
result += count_n[2] * count_m[3]  # (2,3)
result += count_n[3] * count_m[2]  # (3,2)
result += count_n[4] * count_m[1]  # (4,1)

print(result)
```

Let me verify with the first example: n=6, m=12

For n=6:
- r=0: 6//5 = 1 (number: 5)
- r=1: (6-1)//5 + 1 = 5//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 (numbers: 1,6)
- r=2: (6-2)//5 + 1 = 4//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 (number: 2)
- r=3: (6-3)//5 + 1 = 3//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 (number: 3)
- r=4: (6-4)//5 + 1 = 2//5 + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 (number: 4)

For m=12:
- r=0: 12//5 = 2 (numbers: 5,10)
- r=1: (12-1)//5 + 1 = 11//5 + 1 = 2 + 1 = 3 (numbers: 1,6,11)
- r=2: (12-2)//5 + 1 = 10//5 + 1 = 2 + 1 = 3 (numbers: 2,7,12)
- r=3: (12-3)//5 + 1 = 9//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 (numbers: 3,8)
- r=4: (12-4)//5 + 1 = 8//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 (numbers: 4,9)

Now calculate pairs:
- (0,0): 1 * 2 = 2
- (1,4): 2 * 2 = 4  
- (2,3): 1 * 2 = 2
- (3,2): 1 * 3 = 3
- (4,1): 1 * 3 = 3

Total: 2 + 4 + 2 + 3 + 3 = 14 ✓

It matches the expected output!

```python
    else:
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m  
- (x + y) % 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need (x + y) ≡ 0 (mod 5), which means:
- x ≡ (5 - y) (mod 5)

Since we're working mod 5, the possible remainders are 0, 1, 2, 3, 4.

For each possible remainder r of x when divided by 5, we need y to have remainder (5 - r) mod 5.

Let me think about this differently:
- If x ≡ 0 (mod 5), then y ≡ 0 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 1 (mod 5), then y ≡ 4 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 2 (mod 5), then y ≡ 3 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 3 (mod 5), then y ≡ 2 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 4 (mod 5), then y ≡ 1 (mod 5)

## Step-by-step approach

1. Count how many numbers from 1 to n have each remainder when divided by 5
2. Count how many numbers from 1 to m have each remainder when divided by 5
3. For each remainder r, multiply the count of numbers with remainder r in first column by count of numbers with remainder (5-r) mod 5 in second column
4. Sum all these products

## Counting remainders

For any range [1, k], the count of numbers with remainder r (where 0 ≤ r ≤ 4) is:
- If r = 0: floor(k/5)
- If r > 0: floor((k-1)/5) + 1 (if k ≥ r, otherwise 0)

Actually, let me be more precise:
- Numbers with remainder 0: floor(k/5)
- Numbers with remainder 1: floor((k-1)/5) + 1 if k ≥ 1, else 0
- Numbers with remainder 2: floor((k-2)/5) + 1 if k ≥ 2, else 0
- And so on...

But we can simplify: for remainder r (0 ≤ r ≤ 4), the count is:
- floor((k - r) / 5) + 1, if k ≥ r, otherwise 0

Wait, let me verify:
- For remainder 0: floor((k-0)/5) = floor(k/5) ✓
- For remainder 1: floor((k-1)/5) + 1, which gives wrong result for k=1

Actually, the correct count for numbers in [1,k] with remainder r is:
- If k < r: 0
- Otherwise: floor((k - r) / 5) + 1

Let me verify with k=6, r=1:
- Numbers: 1,2,3,4,5,6
- Remainders: 1,2,3,4,0,1
- Remainder 1 appears at positions 1 and 6
- (6-1)/5 = 5/5 = 1, +1 = 2 ✓

For r=0: (6-0)/5 = 6/5 = 1, +1 = 2 (numbers 5, but 5 is remainder 0, actually only 5). Wait, should be 1.

For k=6, numbers with remainder 0: only 5
- (6-0)/5 = 1.2, floor = 1, +1 = 2... wrong.

Let me reconsider. The numbers with remainder r are: r, r+5, r+10, ... ≤ k

So count = floor((k - r) / 5) + 1 if k ≥ r, else 0

For r=0, k=6: (6-0)/5 = 6/5 = 1.2, floor = 1, +1 = 2. But only 5 has remainder 0 (since 10 > 6). Wait, 5 has remainder 0? 5%5 = 0, yes. What about 0? 0 is not in range [1,6]. So only 5.

Actually, when r=0, we have numbers: 5, 10, 15, ... so numbers like 5,10,... ≤ k
So for r=0: floor(k/5)

For r>0: numbers are r, r+5, r+10, ... ≤ k, so floor((k-r)/5) + 1 (if k≥r)

For k=6, r=0: floor(6/5) = 1 ✓
For k=6, r=1: floor((6-1)/5) + 1 = floor(5/5) + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓ (numbers 1,6)
For k=6, r=2: floor((6-2)/5) + 1 = floor(4/5) + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (number 2)
For k=6, r=3: floor((6-3)/5) + 1 = floor(3/5) + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (number 3)
For k=6, r=4: floor((6-4)/5) + 1 = floor(2/5) + 1 = 0 + 1 = 1 ✓ (number 4)

Perfect!

## Algorithm

1. For n: count how many numbers in [1,n] have remainder 0,1,2,3,4
2. For m: count how many numbers in [1,m] have remainder 0,1,2,3,4  
3. For each remainder r in [0,4]:
   - If r = 0: pairs += count_n[r] * count_m[(5-r)%5]
   - Else: pairs += count_n[r] * count_m[(5-r)%5]
4. Return pairs

Since (5-r)%5 = (5-r) when r≠0, and 0 when r=0.

So we can just do: count_n[i] * count_m[(5-i)%5] for i from 0 to 4.

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

def count_remainder(k, r):
    if k < r:
        return 0
    return (k - r) // 5 + 1

# Count remainders for n
count_n = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_n[r] = count_remainder(n, r)

# Count remainders for m
count_m = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_m[r] = count_remainder(m, r)

# Calculate total pairs
total_pairs = 0
for r in range(5):
    needed_remainder = (5 - r) % 5
    total_pairs += count_n[r] * count_m[needed_remainder]

print(total_pairs)
```

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

def count_remainder(k, r):
    if r == 0:
        return k // 5
    else:
        return (k - r) // 5 + 1

# Count remainders for n
count_n = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_n[r] = count_remainder(n, r)

# Count remainders for m

    count_m[r] = count_remainder(m, r)

# Calculate total pairs

for r in range(5):
    needed_remainder = (5 - r) % 5
    total_pairs += count_n[r] * count_m[needed_remainder]
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need:
- x mod 5 + y mod 5 ≡ 0 (mod 5)

This means:
- If x mod 5 = 0, then y mod 5 = 0
- If x mod 5 = 1, then y mod 5 = 4  
- If x mod 5 = 2, then y mod 5 = 3
- If x mod 5 = 3, then y mod 5 = 2
- If x mod 5 = 4, then y mod 5 = 1

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Count numbers in range [1, n] with each remainder mod 5**
   - Count of numbers with remainder r: floor((n - r) / 5) + 1 (if r ≤ n, else 0)
   - Actually: count of numbers with remainder r = (n + 5 - r) // 5

2. **Count numbers in range [1, m] with each remainder mod 5**
   - Similarly for m

3. **Pair the remainders that sum to 0 mod 5**
   - Pair remainder 0 with remainder 0
   - Pair remainder 1 with remainder 4
   - Pair remainder 2 with remainder 3

4. **Sum up all valid pairs**

- r=1: (12-1)//5 + 1 = 11//5 + 1 = 2+1 = 3 (y=1,6,11)
- r=2: (12-2)//5 + 1 = 10//5 + 1 = 2+1 = 3 (y=2,7,12)
- r=3: (12-3)//5 + 1 = 9//5 + 1 = 1+1 = 2 (y=3,8)
- r=4: (12-4)//5 + 1 = 8//5 + 1 = 1+1 = 2 (y=4,9)

Now pair:
- r=0: 1 * 2 = 2
- r=1: 2 * 2 = 4
- r=2: 1 * 2 = 2
- r=3: 1 * 3 = 3
- r=4: 1 * 3 = 3

Sum: 2+4+2+3+3 = 14 ✓

Perfect!

So the formula is correct.

Now I need to implement it efficiently.

For any k, count of numbers in [1,k] with remainder r mod 5:
- If r == 0: k // 5
- If r > 0: (k - r) // 5 + 1

In Python, (k - r) // 5 + 1 works because if k < r, (k-r)//5 is negative, and (k-r)//5 + 1 ≤ 0, but when k < r, there are no numbers with remainder r in [1,k], so count should be 0.

Wait, for example k=3, r=4: (3-4)//5 + 1 = -1//5 + 1 = -1 + 1 = 0 ✓

So it works!

Therefore, we can write a function or just compute directly.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count numbers in [1,n] with each remainder mod 5
count_x = [0] * 5
count_x[0] = n // 5
for r in range(1, 5):
    count_x[r] = (n - r) // 5 + 1

# Count numbers in [1,m] with each remainder mod 5
count_y = [0] * 5
count_y[0] = m // 5
for r in range(1, 5):
    count_y[r] = (m - r) // 5 + 1

# Sum pairs where (x mod 5 + y mod 5) % 5 == 0
result = 0
result += count_x[0] * count_y[0]  # (0,0)
result += count_x[1] * count_y[4]  # (1,4)
result += count_x[2] * count_y[3]  # (2,3)
result += count_x[3] * count_y[2]  # (3,2)
result += count_x[4] * count_y[1]  # (4,1)

print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m  
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need (x + y) ≡ 0 (mod 5), which means y ≡ (-x) (mod 5).

Since we're working mod 5, we can think about the remainders when numbers are divided by 5.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Remainders modulo 5**: Any number has a remainder of 0, 1, 2, 3, or 4 when divided by 5.

2. **Complementary pairs**: For a given remainder r of x when divided by 5, we need y to have remainder (5-r) mod 5 to make their sum divisible by 5.

   - If x mod 5 = 0, we need y mod 5 = 0
   - If x mod 5 = 1, we need y mod 5 = 4
   - If x mod 5 = 2, we need y mod 5 = 3
   - If x mod 5 = 3, we need y mod 5 = 2
   - If x mod 5 = 4, we need y mod 5 = 1

3. **Count numbers with each remainder**:
   - In range [1, n], count how many numbers have remainder r when divided by 5
   - In range [1, m], count how many numbers have remainder s when divided by 5

4. **Calculate pairs**:
   - For remainder 0: count_0_in_n × count_0_in_m
   - For remainder 1: count_1_in_n × count_4_in_m
   - For remainder 2: count_2_in_n × count_3_in_m
   - For remainder 3: count_3_in_n × count_2_in_m
   - For remainder 4: count_4_in_n × count_1_in_m

## Counting numbers with specific remainders

For a range [1, k]:
- Numbers with remainder 0: k // 5
- Numbers with remainder 1: (k + 4) // 5
- Numbers with remainder 2: (k + 3) // 5
- Numbers with remainder 3: (k + 2) // 5
- Numbers with remainder 4: (k + 1) // 5

Actually, let me verify this with examples:
- For k=6: numbers are 1,2,3,4,5,6
- Remainder 0: 5 → count = 6//5 = 1 ✓
- Remainder 1: 1,6 → count = (6+4)//5 = 10//5 = 2 ✓
- Remainder 2: 2 → count = (6+3)//5 = 9//5 = 1 ✓
- Remainder 3: 3 → count = (6+2)//5 = 8//5 = 1 ✓
- Remainder 4: 4 → count = (6+1)//5 = 7//5 = 1 ✓

Wait, that's wrong. For remainder 4: 4 → only one (4), but (6+1)//5 = 7//5 = 1 ✓. That's correct.

Actually, the formula is:
- count(r) in [1,k] = (k - r) // 5 + 1 if r ≤ k, otherwise 0
- But a simpler formula: count(r) = (k + (5-r)) // 5

Let me check: for r=1, k=6: (6+4)//5 = 10//5 = 2 ✓
for r=2, k=6: (6+3)//5 = 9//5 = 1 ✓
for r=3, k=6: (6+2)//5 = 8//5 = 1 ✓
for r=4, k=6: (6+1)//5 = 7//5 = 1 ✓

Actually, this works because the numbers with remainder r are: r, r+5, r+10, ... until ≤ k.

So count = floor((k - r) / 5) + 1 if r ≤ k, else 0.

But we can write it as: (k - r + 5) // 5 if r ≤ k, else 0.

Actually, it's simpler: count(r, k) = (k + 5 - r) // 5

Let me verify: r=1, k=6: (6+5-1)//5 = 10//5 = 2 ✓
r=5, k=10: (10+5-5)//5 = 10//5 = 2 ✓ (numbers: 5,10)

But wait, for r=5, remainder 0: numbers 5,10,15... so count = k//5.

For r=0, we need count = k//5.

For r=1, count = (k-1)//5 + 1 = (k+4)//5
For r=2, count = (k-2)//5 + 1 = (k+3)//5
For r=3, count = (k-3)//5 + 1 = (k+2)//5
For r=4, count = (k-4)//5 + 1 = (k+1)//5

So the general formula is: count(r, k) = (k + (5-r)) // 5 for r = 1,2,3,4
And count(0, k) = k // 5

But (k + (5-r)) // 5 for r=0 would be (k+5)//5 = k//5 + 1, which is wrong.

So we need to handle r=0 separately, or use a unified formula.

Actually, for any remainder r (0,1,2,3,4):
count(r, k) = (k - r + 5) // 5 if r > 0, but for r=0 it's k//5.

Wait, let me recalculate:
- r=0: numbers 5,10,15... → count = k // 5
- r=1: numbers 1,6,11... → count = (k-1)//5 + 1 = (k+4)//5  
- r=2: numbers 2,7,12... → count = (k-2)//5 + 1 = (k+3)//5
- r=3: numbers 3,8,13... → count = (k-3)//5 + 1 = (k+2)//5
- r=4: numbers 4,9,14... → count = (k-4)//5 + 1 = (k+1)//5

So we can write:
- count(0, k) = k // 5
- count(1, k) = (k + 4) // 5
- count(2, k) = (k + 3) // 5
- count(3, k) = (k + 2) // 5
- count(4, k) = (k + 1) // 5

## Final Algorithm

1. Count how many numbers from 1 to n have remainder 0,1,2,3,4 when divided by 5
2. Count how many numbers from 1 to m have remainder 0,1,2,3,4 when divided by 5
3. For each remainder r in 0,1,2,3,4:
   - If r == 0: pairs += count_r_n * count_0_m
   - If r == 1: pairs += count_1_n * count_4_m
   - If r == 2: pairs += count_2_n * count_3_m
   - If r == 3: pairs += count_3_n * count_2_m
   - If r == 4: pairs += count_4_n * count_1_m

Wait, let me think again. We need (x+y) ≡ 0 (mod 5), so:
- If x ≡ 0 (mod 5), we need y ≡ 0 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 1 (mod 5), we need y ≡ 4 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 2 (mod 5), we need y ≡ 3 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 3 (mod 5), we need y ≡ 2 (mod 5)
- If x ≡ 4 (mod 5), we need y ≡ 1 (mod 5)

So:
pairs = count_0_n * count_0_m + count_1_n * count_4_m + count_2_n * count_3_m + count_3_n * count_2_m + count_4_n * count_1_m

Pairs:
- r=0: 1 * 2 = 2
- r=1: 2 * 2 = 4  
- r=2: 1 * 2 = 2
- r=3: 1 * 3 = 3
- r=4: 1 * 3 = 3

Total = 2+4+2+3+3 = 14 ✓

Perfect!

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count frequencies of remainders in [1, n] and [1, m]
def count_remainder(k, r):
    if r == 0:
        return k // 5
    else:
        return (k + 5 - r) // 5

# Count for n
count_n = [0] * 5
count_n[0] = n // 5
count_n[1] = (n + 4) // 5
count_n[2] = (n + 3) // 5
count_n[3] = (n + 2) // 5
count_n[4] = (n + 1) // 5

# Count for m
count_m = [0] * 5
count_m[0] = m // 5
count_m[1] = (m + 4) // 5
count_m[2] = (m + 3) // 5
count_m[3] = (m + 2) // 5
count_m[4] = (m + 1) // 5

# Calculate pairs
result = 0
result += count_n[0] * count_m[0]  # (0,0)
result += count_n[1] * count_m[4]  # (1,4)
result += count_n[2] * count_m[3]  # (2,3)
result += count_n[3] * count_m[2]  # (3,2)
result += count_n[4] * count_m[1]  # (4,1)

print(result)
```

A classroom in a school has six rows with 3 desks in each row. Two people can use the same desk: one sitting on the left and one sitting on the right.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row (left, center, right)
2. Each desk can hold 2 people (left and right seats)
3. The rows are ordered from top (closest to blackboard) to bottom (farthest)
4. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya
5. Convenience is determined by distance from the blackboard - closer rows are more convenient
6. We need to mark one of the most convenient vacant places with 'P'

## Key Insights

1. The top row (row 0) is closest to the blackboard and most convenient
2. The bottom row (row 5) is farthest from the blackboard and least convenient
3. We should prioritize positions from top to bottom
4. Among positions in the same row, we need to consider the specific seats (left, center, right)
5. However, the problem states that convenience is primarily based on row distance from blackboard, with no mention of seat position importance
6. The examples show that when multiple positions have the same convenience level, any of them can be chosen

## Solution Approach

1. Read the 6 lines of input
2. Go through each row from top (row 0) to bottom (row 5)
3. For each row, check each of the three seats (left, center, right)
4. Find the first vacant seat (where the character is '.')
5. Since we want the most convenient place and rows closer to blackboard are more convenient, we should choose the first vacant seat we find in the topmost row
6. Mark that seat with 'P' and output the modified classroom layout

Wait, let me reconsider the examples:

In Example 1: 
- First two rows have all seats occupied
- Row 2 has center and right occupied, left is vacant
- Row 3 has left vacant
- So the most convenient vacant place is the left seat in row 3 (which is the 4th row)

Actually, looking at the output: 
`..-P.-..` - this is row 3 (index 3) with left seat marked as 'P'

In Example 2:
- Row 4 has right seat vacant and is marked with 'P'
- Row 4 is index 4

The problem states "the maximum convenience is 2", which suggests that convenience is determined by row position (with 0 being most convenient).

Actually, let me check: the first example has maximum convenience 3. Since row 0 has convenience 6, row 1 has 5, etc. 

Wait, the problem says "the desks in the top row are the closest to the blackboard" and "maximum convenience is 3" in first example.

Let me rethink: maybe the convenience score is the row number (0 is most convenient). But in example 1, maximum convenience is 3, which would mean row 3 is the most convenient.

Actually, looking at the input and output:
- Input row 3: "..-..-.." -> output: "..-P.-.."
- This is row 3 (index 3) which is the 4th row from top

But row 0,1,2 have some occupied seats. Row 3 has left seat vacant.

I think the convenience is determined by row proximity to blackboard, so we want the row that is closest to blackboard among all vacant seats.

So algorithm:
1. Read all 6 rows
2. Go through rows from 0 (closest) to 5 (farthest)
3. For each row, check each seat (left, center, right) in order
4. The first vacant seat found in any row will be the most convenient
5. Mark that seat with 'P' and output

This makes sense because row 0 is closest to blackboard (most convenient), then row 1, etc.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read 6 lines of input
2. Process each row from top (index 0) to bottom (index 5)
3. For each row, split it into left seat (index 0,1), aisle (index 2), center seat (index 3,4), aisle (index 5), right seat (index 6,7)
4. Check if each seat is vacant ('.')
5. When we find the first vacant seat, mark it with 'P' and break
6. Output the modified classroom

```python
# Read input
classroom = []
for _ in range(6):
    classroom.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place (closest to blackboard)
# Go row by row from top (0) to bottom (5)
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = classroom[row_idx]
    # Extract seats: positions 0,1 for left, 3,4 for center, 6,7 for right
    # The string is: [left1][left2]-[center1][center2]-[right1][right2]
    
    # Check left seat (positions 0-1)
    if row[0] == '.' or row[1] == '.':
        # Find the first vacant seat in left area
        if row[0] == '.':
            # Mark left seat at position 0 with 'P'
            classroom[row_idx] = row[:0] + 'P' + row[1:]  # This is wrong
            # Actually need to fix the string correctly
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A classroom in a school has six rows with 3 desks in each row. Two people can use the same desk: one sitting on the left and one sitting on the right. 

Some places are already occupied, and some places are vacant. Petya has just entered the class and wants to occupy the most convenient place. The conveniences of the places are shown on the picture:

<image>

Here, the desks in the top row are the closest to the blackboard, while the desks in the bottom row are the furthest from the blackboard.

You are given a plan of the class, where '*' denotes an occupied place, '.' denotes a vacant place, and the aisles are denoted by '-'. 

Find any of the most convenient vacant places for Petya.

Input

The input consists of 6 lines. Each line describes one row of desks, starting from the closest to the blackboard. Each line is given in the following format: two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the left desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the center desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the right desk in the current row. So, the length of each of the six lines is 8.

It is guaranteed that there is at least one vacant place in the classroom.

Output

Print the plan of the classroom after Petya takes one of the most convenient for him places. Mark this place with the letter 'P'. There should be exactly one letter 'P' in the plan. Petya can only take a vacant place. In all other places the output should coincide with the input.

If there are multiple answers, print any.

Examples

Input

..-**-..
..-**-..
..-..-..
..-..-..
..-..-..
..-..-..


Output

..-**-..
..-**-..
..-..-..
..-P.-..
..-..-..
..-..-..

**-**-**
..-**-.*
**-**-**
..-..-..
..-**-..


Output

**-**-**
**-**-**

**-**-**

..-**-..

..-..-..
..-**-..


Output

*.-*P-**
**-**-**
**-**-**
..-..-..
..-**-..

Note

In the first example the maximum convenience is 3.

In the second example the maximum convenience is 2.

In the third example the maximum convenience is 4.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row (left, center, right)
2. Each desk can hold 2 people (left and right seats)
3. Rows are numbered from top (closest to blackboard) to bottom (farthest from blackboard)
4. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya
5. Convenience is determined by distance from the blackboard - closer rows have higher convenience
6. Among vacant places in the most convenient row, we choose any (the problem says "find any")

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 6 lines of 8 characters each
- Each line has format: `LL-CC-RR` where L and C and R are either '*' or '.'

### Step 2: Determine convenience
- The top row (index 0) is closest to blackboard (highest convenience)
- The bottom row (index 5) is farthest from blackboard (lowest convenience)
- So row 0 has convenience 0 (closest), row 1 has convenience 1, etc.

### Step 3: Find the most convenient vacant place
- Start from row 0 (closest to blackboard)
- Check each position (left, center, right) in that row
- If a position is vacant ('.'), this is a candidate
- Since we're looking for the most convenient place, we should pick the first vacant place we find in the topmost row
- If there are no vacant places in row 0, move to row 1, and so on

### Step 4: Output the result
- Create a copy of the input
- Replace the chosen vacant place with 'P'
- Print the modified classroom layout

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from the top row (closest to blackboard, row 0)
for row_idx in range(6):
    # Check left desk (positions 0 and 1)
    if rows[row_idx][0] == '.':
        # Found a vacant left seat - this has the highest convenience
        # Mark it with 'P' and break
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][1] == '.':
        # Found a vacant center seat
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][5] == '.':
        # Found a vacant right seat
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:5] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][6:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][6] == '.':
        # Found a vacant right seat
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:6] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][7:]
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

Actually, I see the issue - when I do `rows[row_idx][7:]`, that's wrong. The string is only 8 characters long, so indices go from 0 to 7.

When I replace position 7, I should do `rows[row_idx][:7] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][8:]`, but rows[row_idx][8:] is empty since there's no index 8.

So for position 7, it should be `rows[row_idx][:7] + 'P'`.

But when I do `rows[row_idx][7:]`, that's correct - index 7 is the last character.

Actually, let me test with a simple example:
- "abcde" - indices 0,1,2,3,4
- [:3] + 'x' + [4:] would be "abcx" + "de" = "abcxde" - wrong!

I need to be more careful. The correct way is:

For position i, replace with 'P':
- new_string = rows[row_idx][0:i] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][i+1:]

So for position 0: rows[row_idx][0:0] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][1:] = '' + 'P' + rest
For position 1: rows[row_idx][0:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]

Let me fix:

```python

for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place starting from top row
for row_idx in range(6):
    # Check left desk (positions 0, 1)
    if rows[row_idx][0] == '.':
        # Replace position 0 with 'P'

        break

        # Replace position 1 with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    # Check center desk (positions 3, 4)
    elif rows[row_idx][3] == '.':
        # Replace position 3 with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:3] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][4:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][4] == '.':
        # Replace position 4 with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:4] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][5:]
        break

```

Actually, rows[row_idx][8:] will be empty and cause an error. The string is only 8 characters long, so indices go from 0 to 7.

For position 7: rows[row_idx][0:7] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][8:] 
But rows[row_idx][8:] is out of bounds.

I should just use:
```
rows[row_idx][0:i] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][i+1:]
```

For i=7: that's rows[row_idx][0:7] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][8:] - but rows[row_idx][8:] is empty since length is 8.

Since the string length is 8, for position 7, `[7+1:]` is `[8:]` which is empty.

This should work in Python since it will slice from 8 to end, which is empty.

But to be safe, let me verify: in Python, `s[8:]` for a string of length 8 is empty.

So it's actually correct.

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place (starting from top row)
for row_idx in range(6):
    # Check left desk positions 0 and 1
    if rows[row_idx][0] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:0] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][1:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][1] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    # Check center desk positions 3 and 4
    elif rows[row_idx][3] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:3] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][4:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][4] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:4] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][5:]
        break
    # Check right desk positions 6 and 7
    elif rows[row_idx][6] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:6] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][7:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][7] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][0:7] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][8:]
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

This should work correctly. The most convenient place is in the closest row to the blackboard (row 0), and we check the positions in order (left, center, right). Since the problem says "print any", we just need to find the first vacant place in the most convenient row.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A classroom in a school has six rows with 3 desks in each row. Two people can use the same desk: one sitting on the left and one sitting on the right. 

Some places are already occupied, and some places are vacant. Petya has just entered the class and wants to occupy the most convenient place. The conveniences of the places are shown on the picture:

<image>

Here, the desks in the top row are the closest to the blackboard, while the desks in the bottom row are the furthest from the blackboard.

You are given a plan of the class, where '*' denotes an occupied place, '.' denotes a vacant place, and the aisles are denoted by '-'. 

Find any of the most convenient vacant places for Petya.

Input

The input consists of 6 lines. Each line describes one row of desks, starting from the closest to the blackboard. Each line is given in the following format: two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the left desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the center desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the right desk in the current row. So, the length of each of the six lines is 8.

It is guaranteed that there is at least one vacant place in the classroom.

Output

Print the plan of the classroom after Petya takes one of the most convenient for him places. Mark this place with the letter 'P'. There should be exactly one letter 'P' in the plan. Petya can only take a vacant place. In all other places the output should coincide with the input.

If there are multiple answers, print any.

Examples

Input

..-**-..
..-**-..
..-..-..
..-..-..
..-..-..
..-..-..


Output

..-**-..
..-**-..
..-..-..
..-P.-..
..-..-..
..-..-..


Input

**-**-**
**-**-**
..-**-.*
**-**-**
..-..-..
..-**-..


Output

**-**-**

..-P.-..
..-**-..

**-**-**

..-..-..
..-**-..


Output

**-**-*.
*.-*P-**
**-**-**
**-**-**
..-..-..
..-**-..

Note

In the first example the maximum convenience is 3.

In the second example the maximum convenience is 2.

In the third example the maximum convenience is 4.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row (left, center, right)
2. Each desk can accommodate 2 people (left and right seats)
3. The rows are numbered from top (closest to blackboard) to bottom (farthest)
4. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya
5. Convenience is determined by distance from the blackboard - top rows are more convenient
6. We output the classroom plan with 'P' marking Petya's chosen spot

## Key Insights

1. The convenience decreases as we go from top row (row 0) to bottom row (row 5)
2. Among the same row, all positions have equal convenience
3. We should prioritize the topmost available row, and within that row, any available seat will do
4. Since we're asked for "any" most convenient place, we just need to find the first available seat in the topmost row

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read the 6 lines of input
2. Process each row from top to bottom (row 0 to row 5)
3. For each row, check if any of the 3 desks (left, center, right) has a vacant spot ('.')
4. Return the first vacant spot we find in the topmost row
5. Replace that '.' with 'P' and output the modified plan

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from top row (row 0) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    # Check left desk (positions 0, 1)
    if row[0] == '.' or row[1] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in this row
        # Create new row with 'P' at the first vacant position
        new_row = list(row)
        if row[0] == '.':
            new_row[0] = 'P'
        else:
            new_row[1] = 'P'
        # Replace the original row with new one
        rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
        break
    # Check center desk (positions 3, 4)
    elif row[3] == '.' or row[4] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in this row
        new_row = list(row)
        if row[3] == '.':
            new_row[3] = 'P'
        else:
            new_row[4] = 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
        break
    # Check right desk (positions 5, 6)
    elif row[5] == '.' or row[6] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in this row
        new_row = list(row)
        if row[5] == '.':
            new_row[5] = 'P'
        else:
            new_row[6] = 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

Wait, I think I made an error in the position indexing. Let me reconsider the format:

Each row is in format: "LL-CC-RR" where:
- Positions 0,1: left desk (two characters)
- Position 2: aisle
- Positions 3,4: center desk (two characters)
- Position 5: aisle
- Positions 6,7: right desk (two characters)

So the positions are:
- Left desk: indices 0,1
- Center desk: indices 3,4  
- Right desk: indices 6,7

Let me correct this:

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from top row (row 0) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    # Check left desk (positions 0, 1)
    if row[0] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in left desk, place P at position 0
        rows[row_idx] = row[:1] + 'P' + row[2:]
        break
    elif row[1] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in left desk, place P at position 1
        rows[row_idx] = row[:1] + 'P' + row[2:]
        break
    # Check center desk (positions 3, 4)
    elif row[3] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in center desk, place P at position 3
        rows[row_idx] = row[:3] + 'P' + row[4:]
        break
    elif row[4] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in center desk, place P at position 4
        rows[row_idx] = row[:3] + 'P' + row[4:]
        break
    # Check right desk (positions 6, 7)
    elif row[6] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in right desk, place P at position 6
        rows[row_idx] = row[:6] + 'P' + row[7:]
        break
    elif row[7] == '.':
        # Found a vacant seat in right desk, place P at position 7
        rows[row_idx] = row[:6] + 'P' + row[7:]
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

Actually, I can simplify this further by checking all positions in order from top to bottom:

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the first available position in the most convenient (topmost) row
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    # Check positions in order: left (0,1), center (3,4), right (6,7)
    if row[0] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:1] + 'P' + row[2:]
        break
    elif row[1] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:2] + 'P' + row[3:]
        break
    elif row[3] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:3] + 'P' + row[4:]
        break
    elif row[4] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:4] + 'P' + row[5:]
        break
    elif row[6] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:6] + 'P' + row[7:]
        break
    elif row[7] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:7] + 'P' + row[8:]
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row
2. Each desk has a left and right seat (so 2 people per desk)
3. Desks are arranged with aisles in between
4. The rows are numbered from top (closest to blackboard) to bottom (farthest)
5. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya
6. Convenience is determined by distance from the blackboard - closer rows are more convenient
7. We need to mark one of the most convenient vacant places with 'P'

## Key Insights

1. The convenience is determined by row number - row 0 (top) is most convenient
2. We should prioritize finding vacant places in the top rows first
3. If there are multiple vacant places in the same row, any of them is acceptable
4. We need to find the first (by row number) vacant seat in the topmost available row

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read the 6 lines of input
2. For each row from top (row 0) to bottom (row 5):
   - Check if there's any vacant place (either left, center, or right seat)
   - If found, mark that place with 'P' and break
3. Output the modified classroom plan

## Implementation Details

Each row has the format: `LL-CC-RR` where:
- LL: left-left and left-right seats
- - : aisle
- CC: center-left and center-right seats  
- - : aisle
- RR: right-left and right-right seats

So the positions are:
- Position 0: left-left seat
- Position 1: left-right seat
- Position 2: aisle
- Position 3: center-left seat
- Position 4: center-right seat
- Position 5: aisle
- Position 6: right-left seat
- Position 7: right-right seat

We need to check from row 0 (top) to row 5 (bottom) and find the first vacant seat.

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place (starting from top row)
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    # Check each position in the row
    if row[0] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[0] + row[1] + row[2] + row[3] + row[4] + row[5] + row[6] + row[7]
        rows[row_idx] = row[0] + 'P' + row[2] + row[3] + row[4] + row[5] + row[6] + row[7]
        break
    elif row[1] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[0] + 'P' + row[2] + row[3] + row[4] + row[5] + row[6] + row[7]
        break
    elif row[3] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[0] + row[1] + 'P' + row[3] + row[4] + row[5] + row[6] + row[7]
        break
    elif row[4] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[0] + row[1] + row[2] + 'P' + row[4] + row[5] + row[6] + row[7]
        break
    elif row[6] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[0] + row[1] + row[2] + row[3] + row[4] + row[5] + 'P' + row[7]
        break
    elif row[7] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[0] + row[1] + row[2] + row[3] + row[4] + row[5] + row[6] + 'P'
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row (left, center, right)
2. Each desk can hold 2 people (left and right seats)
3. The rows are numbered from 0 (closest to blackboard) to 5 (farthest from blackboard)
4. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya
5. Convenience is determined by distance from the blackboard - closer rows are more convenient
6. We need to output the classroom plan with 'P' marking Petya's chosen seat

## Key Insights

1. The most convenient places are in the top rows (closest to blackboard)
2. We should prioritize rows from 0 to 5 (top to bottom)
3. Within each row, we should check left, center, right seats from left to right (since no specific order is given in the problem)
4. We need to find the first vacant seat (in order of convenience)

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read the 6 lines of input
2. Parse each row to identify positions of left, center, and right desks
3. Check seats from row 0 (closest) to row 5 (farthest)
4. In each row, check left desk (positions 0,1), center desk (positions 3,4), right desk (positions 5,6)
5. For each position, check if it's vacant ('.')
6. When we find the first vacant seat in the most convenient row, mark it with 'P'
7. Output the modified classroom plan

## Implementation

```python
# Read the 6 lines of input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    row = input().strip()
    rows.append(row)

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from row 0 (closest to blackboard) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    
    # Check left desk (positions 0,1)
    if row[0] == '.':
        # We found a vacant left seat - this is the most convenient
        # Mark it with 'P' and break
        rows[row_idx] = row[:1] + 'P' + row[2:]
        break
    elif row[1] == '.':
        # We found a vacant left seat - this is the most convenient
        rows[row_idx] = row[:1] + 'P' + row[2:]
        break
    elif row[3] == '.':
        # We found a vacant center seat
        rows[row_idx] = row[:3] + 'P' + row[4:]
        break
    elif row[4] == '.':
        # We found a vacant center seat
        rows[row_idx] = row[:3] + 'P' + row[4:]
        break
    elif row[5] == '.':
        # We found a vacant right seat
        rows[row_idx] = row[:5] + 'P' + row[6:]
        break
    elif row[6] == '.':
        # We found a vacant right seat
        rows[row_idx] = row[:5] + 'P' + row[6:]
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

 So final solution: find the smallest row index with a vacant seat, and among seats in that row, choose the one with the smallest position index.

```python
# Read the 6 lines of input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    row = input().strip()
    rows.append(row)

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from row 0 (closest to blackboard) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    
    # Check left desk (positions 0, 1)
    if row[0] == '.':
        # Mark left seat with P (first position)
        rows[row_idx] = row[:1] + 'P' + row[2:]
        break
    elif row[1] == '.':
        # Mark left seat with P (second position)
        rows[row_idx] = row[:2] + 'P' + row[3:]
        break
    # Check center desk (positions 3, 4)
    elif row[3] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:3] + 'P' + row[4:]
        break
    elif row[4] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:4] + 'P' + row[5:]
        break
    # Check right desk (positions 6, 7)
    elif row[6] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:6] + 'P' + row[7:]
        break
    elif row[7] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:7] + 'P'
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A classroom in a school has six rows with 3 desks in each row. Two people can use the same desk: one sitting on the left and one sitting on the right. 

Some places are already occupied, and some places are vacant. Petya has just entered the class and wants to occupy the most convenient place. The conveniences of the places are shown on the picture:

<image>

Here, the desks in the top row are the closest to the blackboard, while the desks in the bottom row are the furthest from the blackboard.

You are given a plan of the class, where '*' denotes an occupied place, '.' denotes a vacant place, and the aisles are denoted by '-'. 

Find any of the most convenient vacant places for Petya.

Input

The input consists of 6 lines. Each line describes one row of desks, starting from the closest to the blackboard. Each line is given in the following format: two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the left desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the center desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the right desk in the current row. So, the length of each of the six lines is 8.

It is guaranteed that there is at least one vacant place in the classroom.

Output

Print the plan of the classroom after Petya takes one of the most convenient for him places. Mark this place with the letter 'P'. There should be exactly one letter 'P' in the plan. Petya can only take a vacant place. In all other places the output should coincide with the input.

If there are multiple answers, print any.

Examples

Input

..-**-..
..-**-..
..-..-..
..-..-..
..-..-..
..-..-..


Output

..-**-..
..-**-..
..-..-..
..-P.-..
..-..-..
..-..-..


Input

**-**-**
**-**-**
..-**-.*
**-**-**
..-..-..
..-**-..


Output

**-**-**
**-**-**
..-**-.*
**-**-**
..-P.-..
..-**-..


Input

**-**-*.
*.-*.-**
**-**-**
**-**-**
..-..-..
..-**-..


Output

**-**-*.
*.-*P-**
**-**-**
**-**-**
..-..-..
..-**-..

Note

In the first example the maximum convenience is 3.

In the second example the maximum convenience is 2.

In the third example the maximum convenience is 4.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row (left, center, right)
2. Each desk can hold 2 people (left and right seats)
3. The convenience is determined by distance from the blackboard - top row is closest (convenience = 1), bottom row is farthest (convenience = 6)
4. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya and mark it with 'P'
5. The convenience is measured from the blackboard, so row 0 (top) has the highest convenience value (1), row 5 (bottom) has the lowest (6)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 6 lines of input
- Each line has format: "LL-CC-RR" where L and C and R are pairs of characters (each can be '*' or '.')

### Step 2: Determine convenience
- The convenience value is based on the row index: 
  - Row 0 (closest to blackboard) → convenience = 1
  - Row 1 → convenience = 2
  - ...
  - Row 5 (farthest) → convenience = 6
- We want the place with the highest convenience (lowest row index)

### Step 3: Find the most convenient vacant place
- Among all vacant places (those marked with '.'), we need to find the one in the topmost row (smallest row index)
- If multiple places have the same convenience (same row), we can pick any

### Step 4: Output the result
- Create a copy of the input
- Replace the first '.' we find in the topmost available row with 'P'
- Print the result

## Implementation Strategy

1. Read all 6 lines into a list
2. Iterate through rows from top (row 0) to bottom (row 5)
3. For each row, check each position (left, center, right) 
4. Find the first vacant position (marked with '.') in the earliest row
5. Replace that position with 'P'
6. Print the modified classroom layout

```python
# Read the input
classroom = []
for _ in range(6):
    classroom.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Most convenient means closest to blackboard (smallest row index)
for row in range(6):
    # Check left desk (positions 0, 1)
    if classroom[row][0] == '.' or classroom[row][1] == '.':
        # Check left desk first
        if classroom[row][0] == '.':
            classroom[row] = classroom[row][:0] + 'P' + classroom[row][1:]
            break
        elif classroom[row][1] == '.':
            classroom[row] = classroom[row][:1] + 'P' + classroom[row][2:]
            break
    
    # Check center desk (positions 3, 4)
    if classroom[row][3] == '.' or classroom[row][4] == '.':
        if classroom[row][3] == '.':
            classroom[row] = classroom[row][:3] + 'P' + classroom[row][4:]
            break
        elif classroom[row][4] == '.':
            classroom[row] = classroom[row][:4] + 'P' + classroom[row][5:]
            break
    
    # Check right desk (positions 6, 7)
    if classroom[row][6] == '.' or classroom[row][7] == '.':
        if classroom[row][6] == '.':
            classroom[row] = classroom[row][:6] + 'P' + classroom[row][7:]
            break
        elif classroom[row][7] == '.':
            classroom[row] = classroom[row][:7] + 'P' + classroom[row][8:]
            break

# Print the result
for line in classroom:
    print(line)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row (left, center, right)
2. Each desk can hold 2 people (left and right)
3. The rows are numbered from top (closest to blackboard) to bottom (farthest)
4. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya
5. "Convenience" is determined by distance from the blackboard - top rows are more convenient
6. We need to output the classroom plan with 'P' marking Petya's chosen place

## Key Insights

1. The convenience decreases as we move from top row (row 0) to bottom row (row 5)
2. Among places in the same row, there's no explicit ranking mentioned, so we can choose any vacant place in the most convenient row
3. Since top rows are most convenient, we should start checking from row 0 and go down
4. Once we find a vacant place in the most convenient row, we can mark it with 'P' and stop

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read the 6 lines of input
2. Process each row from top (index 0) to bottom (index 5)
3. For each row, check each position (left, center, right) for vacant spaces (represented by '.')
4. As soon as we find a vacant space in the topmost available row, mark it with 'P' and break
5. Output the modified classroom plan

## Implementation Details

Each row has the format: `LL-CC-RR` where:
- L is left desk (2 characters)
- - is aisle
- C is center desk (2 characters) 
- - is aisle
- R is right desk (2 characters)

So the positions are:
- Position 0: left desk, first person
- Position 1: left desk, second person  
- Position 2: aisle
- Position 3: center desk, first person
- Position 4: center desk, second person
- Position 5: aisle
- Position 6: right desk, first person
- Position 7: right desk, second person

We need to check the positions in the order of decreasing convenience (top to bottom), so we start with row 0.

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from top row (row 0) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    # Check each position in the row
    # Positions 0,1: left desk
    if row[0] == '.' or row[1] == '.':
        # Found a vacant space in left desk
        # Replace the first vacant character with 'P'
        new_row = list(row)
        if row[0] == '.':
            new_row[0] = 'P'
            rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
            break
        else:  # row[1] == '.'
            new_row[1] = 'P'
            rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
            break
    
    # Check center desk positions 3,4
    if row[3] == '.' or row[4] == '.':
        new_row = list(row)
        if row[3] == '.':
            new_row[3] = 'P'
            rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
            break
        else:  # row[4] == '.'
            new_row[4] = 'P'
            rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
            break
    
    # Check right desk positions 6,7
    if row[6] == '.' or row[7] == '.':
        new_row = list(row)
        if row[6] == '.':
            new_row[6] = 'P'
            rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
            break
        else:  # row[7] == '.'
            new_row[7] = 'P'
            rows[row_idx] = ''.join(new_row)
            break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the problem. The note says "In the first example the maximum convenience is 3" and "In the second example the maximum convenience is 2" and "In the third example the maximum convenience is 4".

Actually, looking at the examples more carefully, the convenience is clearly based on row position (top row has highest convenience). The problem states "the desks in the top row are the closest to the blackboard", so we should prioritize top rows.

My solution is correct - we start from row 0 and go down, and in each row we check left, center, right desks in order. Since the problem says "find any of the most convenient vacant places", and top rows are more convenient, we should pick the first vacant spot we find in the topmost available row.

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from top row (row 0) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    row = rows[row_idx]
    # Check left desk positions 0,1
    if row[0] == '.':
        # Replace first character with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = row[:0] + 'P' + row[1:]
        break
    elif row[1] == '.':
        # Replace second character with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = row[:1] + 'P' + row[2:]
        break
    # Check center desk positions 3,4
    elif row[3] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:3] + 'P' + row[4:]
        break
    elif row[4] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:4] + 'P' + row[5:]
        break
    # Check right desk positions 6,7
    elif row[6] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:6] + 'P' + row[7:]
        break
    elif row[7] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = row[:7] + 'P' + row[8:]
        break

# Output the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A classroom in a school has six rows with 3 desks in each row. Two people can use the same desk: one sitting on the left and one sitting on the right. 

Some places are already occupied, and some places are vacant. Petya has just entered the class and wants to occupy the most convenient place. The conveniences of the places are shown on the picture:

<image>

Here, the desks in the top row are the closest to the blackboard, while the desks in the bottom row are the furthest from the blackboard.

You are given a plan of the class, where '*' denotes an occupied place, '.' denotes a vacant place, and the aisles are denoted by '-'. 

Find any of the most convenient vacant places for Petya.

Input

The input consists of 6 lines. Each line describes one row of desks, starting from the closest to the blackboard. Each line is given in the following format: two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the left desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the center desk in the current row; a character '-' — the aisle; two characters, each is '*' or '.' — the description of the right desk in the current row. So, the length of each of the six lines is 8.

It is guaranteed that there is at least one vacant place in the classroom.

Output

Print the plan of the classroom after Petya takes one of the most convenient for him places. Mark this place with the letter 'P'. There should be exactly one letter 'P' in the plan. Petya can only take a vacant place. In all other places the output should coincide with the input.

If there are multiple answers, print any.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a classroom with 6 rows and 3 desks per row (left, center, right)
2. Each desk can hold 2 people (left and right seats)
3. The rows are numbered from 0 (closest to blackboard) to 5 (farthest from blackboard)
4. We need to find the most convenient vacant place for Petya
5. Convenience is determined by distance from the blackboard - closer rows are more convenient
6. Among equally convenient rows, we can choose any vacant seat

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 6 lines, each of length 8
- Each line has format: "LL-CC-RR" where L and C are left/center/right desk statuses

### Step 2: Determine convenience
- The convenience increases as we get closer to the blackboard
- Row 0 (first row) is most convenient, row 5 (last row) is least convenient
- We should prioritize the closest available row first

### Step 3: Find the most convenient vacant seat
- Check rows from 0 to 5 (starting with row 0)
- For each row, check the left, center, and right seats
- If a seat is vacant ('.'), it's a candidate
- Since we want the most convenient, we stop at the first available row we find and pick the first available seat in that row

### Step 4: Output the result
- Create a copy of the input
- Replace the first found vacant seat with 'P'
- Print the result

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from the closest row (row 0) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    # Check left desk (positions 0 and 1)
    if rows[row_idx][0] == '.':
        # Found a vacant left seat in this row
        # Replace it with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][1] == '.':
        # Found a vacant center seat in this row
        # Replace it with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][5] == '.':
        # Found a vacant right seat in this row
        # Replace it with 'P'
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:5] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][6:]
        break

# Print the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

Wait, there's still a problem. When I do `rows[row_idx][:6] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][7:]`, this would be adding 'P' at position 6, but I want to replace the character at position 6.

Actually, for right desk, positions 6 and 7 represent the two seats. If position 6 is '.', I want to replace it with 'P'.

So:
- For position 6: replace with 'P': `rows[row_idx][:6] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][7:]`
- For position 7: replace with 'P': `rows[row_idx][:7] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][8:]`

But rows are length 8, so indices go from 0 to 7.

For position 7: `rows[row_idx][:7] + 'P'` would work because the last character is at index 7.

Actually, let me check: if I have a string of length 8, `[:7]` gives indices 0-6, then `+ 'P'` gives index 7, then `+ rows[row_idx][8:]` would be empty because there's no index 8.

But I only need to add one character. The string is 8 characters long, so:
- For index 6: `[:6] + 'P' + [7:]` → this gives us positions 0-5, then 'P', then position 7
- For index 7: `[:7] + 'P'` → this gives us positions 0-6, then 'P' (at position 7)

But this would be wrong because `[7:]` after `[:6]` would include the character at position 7.

Actually, `[7:]` means from index 7 to the end. Since the string is length 8, `[7:]` is just the character at index 7.

So for replacing position 7: I need `[:7] + 'P'` which will put 'P' at position 7.

For replacing position 6: I need `[:6] + 'P' + [7:]` which will put 'P' at position 6 and keep position 7.

Actually, no: if I do `[:6] + 'P' + [7:]`, and [7:] is just the character at position 7, then this will give me:
- 0-5: original
- 6: 'P'  
- 7: original[7]

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from row 0 (closest to blackboard)
for row_idx in range(6):
    # Check left desk (positions 0 and 1)
    if rows[row_idx][0] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][1] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:2] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][3:]
        break
    # Check center desk (positions 3 and 4)
    elif rows[row_idx][3] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:3] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][4:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][4] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:4] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][5:]
        break
    # Check right desk (positions 6 and 7)
    elif rows[row_idx][6] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:6] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][7:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][7] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:7] + 'P'
        break

# Print the result
for row in rows:
    print(row)
```

```python
# Read the input
rows = []
for _ in range(6):
    rows.append(input().strip())

# Find the most convenient vacant place
# Start from the closest row (row 0) and go down
for row_idx in range(6):
    # Check left desk (positions 0 and 1)
    if rows[row_idx][0] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:1] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][2:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][1] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:2] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][3:]
        break
    # Check center desk (positions 3 and 4)
    elif rows[row_idx][3] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:3] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][4:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][4] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:4] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][5:]
        break
    # Check right desk (positions 6 and 7)
    elif rows[row_idx][6] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:6] + 'P' + rows[row_idx][7:]
        break
    elif rows[row_idx][7] == '.':
        rows[row_idx] = rows[row_idx][:7] + 'P'
        break

# Print the result
```
